| 12¶1 fr.434 umn.351-52 | 12. 1227. Traité de nomadologie : la machine de guerre Char nomade entièrement de bois, Altaï, Ve-IVe siècles av. J.-C. Axiome I : La machine de guerre est extérieure à l'appareil d'Etat. Proposition I : Cette extériorité est d'abord attestée par la mythologie, l'épopée, le drame et les jeux. Georges Dumézil, dans des analyses décisives de la mythologie indo-européenne, a montré que la souveraineté politique, ou domination, avait deux têtes : celle du roi-magicien, celle du prêtre-juriste. Rex et flamen, raj et Brahman, Romulus et Numa, Varuna et Mitra, le despote et le législateur, le lieur et l'organisateur. Et sans doute ces deux pôles s'opposent terme à terme, comme l'obscur et le clair, le violent et le calme, le rapide et le grave, le terrible et le réglé, le « lien » et le « pacte », etc. 1. Mais leur opposition n'est que relative ; ils fonctionnent en couple, en alternance, comme s'ils exprimaient une division de l'Un ou composaient eux-mêmes une unité souveraine. « A la fois antithétiques et complémentaires, nécessaires l'un à l'autre et par conséquent sans hostilité, sans mythologie de conflit : chaque spécification sur un des plans appelle mécaniquement une spécification homologue sur l'autre, et, à eux deux, ils épuisent le champ de la fonction. » Ce sont les éléments principaux d'un appareil d'Etat qui procède en Un-Deux, distribue les distinctions binaires et forme un milieu d'intériorité. C'est une double articulation qui fait de l'appareil d'Etat une strate. On remarquera que la guerre n'est pas prise dans cet appareil. | 12. 1227: Treatise on Nomadology: The War Machine Nomad Chariot, Entirely of Wood, Altai, Fifth to Fourth Centuries B.C. AXIOM I. The war machine is exterior to the State apparatus. PROPOSITION I. This exteriority is first attested to in mythology, epic, drama, and games. Georges Dumezil, in his definitive analyses of Indo-European mythology, has shown that political sovereignty, or domination, has two heads: the magician-king and the jurist-priest. Rex and flamen, raj and Brahman, Romulus and Numa, Varuna and Mitra, the despot and the legislator, the binder and the organizer. Undoubtedly, these two poles stand in opposition term by term, as the obscure and the clear, the violent and the calm, the quick and the weighty, the fearsome and the regulated, the "bond" and the "pact," etc.¹ But their opposition is only relative; they function as a pair, in alternation, as though they expressed a division of the One or constituted in themselves a sovereign unity. "At once antithetical and complementary, necessary to one another and consequently without hostility, lacking a mythology of conflict: a specification on any one level automatically calls forth a homologous specification on another. The two together exhaust the field of the function." They are the principal elements of a State apparatus that proceeds by a One-Two, distributes binary distinctions, and forms a milieu of interiority. It is a double articulation that makes the State apparatus into a stratum. It will be noted that war is not contained within this apparatus. | 12. 1227. ***Traité de nomadologie*** <span style="color:green">[Treatise on nomadology – traité: the didactic genre that proceeds by axioms, propositions, and problems; the same word names a treaty]</span>: the war machine. Nomad chariot entirely of wood, Altai, fifth to fourth centuries B.C. Axiom I: The war machine is exterior to the ***appareil d'Etat*** <span style="color:green">[State apparatus – appareil: also machinery, rigging; appareil photo = camera: the workaday mechanical register Massumi's "apparatus" elevates]</span>. Proposition I: This exteriority is first ***attestée*** <span style="color:green">[attested – witnessed, certified: the register of testimony and documents]</span> by mythology, the epic, the drama, and games. Georges Dumézil, in ***décisives*** <span style="color:green">[decisive – Massumi: "definitive", which makes the analyses final where the French makes them turning points]</span> analyses of Indo-European mythology, has shown that political sovereignty, or domination, had two heads: that of the magician-king, that of the priest-jurist. Rex and flamen, raj and Brahman, Romulus and Numa, Varuna and Mitra, the despot and the legislator, the ***lieur*** <span style="color:green">[binder – agent noun of lier, to bind: the bind-chain that runs from liens and nœuds to the Dieux-lieurs of plateau 13]</span> and the organizer. And doubtless these two poles oppose each other term by term, as the obscure and the clear, the violent and the calm, the rapid and the ***grave*** <span style="color:green">[grave, heavy – the register of weight that becomes the State's gravitas; Massumi: "weighty"]</span>, the terrible and the ***réglé*** <span style="color:green">[regulated – set by rule; of an account, settled]</span>, the « ***lien*** <span style="color:green">[bond, tie – from lier: the bind-chain]</span> » and the « pact », etc.¹ But their opposition is only relative; they function as a couple, in alternation, as if they expressed a division of the One or themselves composed a sovereign unity. « At once antithetical and complementary, necessary to one another and consequently without hostility, without a mythology of conflict: each specification on one of the planes mechanically calls up a homologous specification on the other, and, the two of them together, they exhaust the field of the function. » These are the principal elements of an *appareil d'Etat* that proceeds by ***Un-Deux*** <span style="color:green">[One-Two – the binary count; also the drill count, un, deux]</span>, distributes binary distinctions, and forms a ***milieu d'intériorité*** <span style="color:green">[milieu of interiority – milieu: middle, environment, surroundings; here the closed inside the State composes]</span>. It is a double articulation that makes of the *appareil d'Etat* a ***strate*** <span style="color:green">[stratum – geology: a layer laid down and hardened; the concept of plateau 3, "The Geology of Morals"]</span>. ***On remarquera*** <span style="color:green">[one will note – impersonal on + future of instruction: the treatise's demonstrative voice]</span> that war is not ***prise dans*** <span style="color:green">[taken into, caught in – prendre, prise: the verb of seizure itself; Massumi: "contained within", which loses the capture]</span> this *appareil*. |
| 12¶2 fr.434 umn.352 | Ou bien l'Etat dispose d'une violence qui ne passe pas par la guerre : il emploie des policiers et des geôliers plutôt que des guerriers, il n'a pas d'armes et n'en a pas besoin, il agit par capture magique immédiate, il « saisit » et « lie », empêchant tout combat. Ou bien l'Etat acquiert une armée, mais qui présuppose une intégration juridique de la guerre et l'organisation d'une fonction militaire 2. Quant à la machine de guerre en elle-même, elle semble bien irréductible à l'appareil d'Etat, extérieure à sa souveraineté, préalable à son droit : elle vient d'ailleurs. Indra, le dieu guerrier, ne s'oppose pas moins à Varuna qu'à Mitra 3. Il ne se réduit pas à l'un des deux, pas plus qu'il ne forme un troisième. Il serait plutôt comme la multiplicité pure et sans mesure, la meute, irruption de l'éphémère et puissance de la métamorphose. Il dénoue le lien autant qu'il trahit le pacte. Il fait valoir une furor contre la mesure, une célérité contre la gravité, un secret contre le public, une puissance contre la souveraineté, une machine contre l'appareil. Il témoigne d'une autre justice, parfois d'une cruauté incompréhensible, mais parfois 1. Georges Dumézil, Mitra-Varuna, Gallimard (sur le nexum et le mutuum, le lien et le contrat, cf. 118-124). 2. L'Etat, suivant son premier pôle (Varuna, Ouranos, Romulus), opère par lien magique, prise ou capture immédiate : il ne combat pas, et n'a pas de machine de guerre, « il lie, et c'est tout ». Suivant son autre pôle (Mitra, Zeus, Numa), il s'approprie une armée, mais en la soumettant à des règles institutionnelles et juridiques qui n'en font plus qu'une pièce de l'appareil d'Etat : ainsi Mars-Tiwaz n'est pas un dieu guerrier, mais un dieu « juriste de la guerre ». Cf. | Either the State has at its disposal a violence that is not channeled through war: either it uses police officers and jailers in place of warriors, has no arms and no need of them, operates by immediate, magical capture, "seizes" and "binds," preventing all combat - or, the State acquires an army, but in a way that presupposes a juridical integration of war and the organization of a military function.² As for the war machine in itself, it seems to be irreducible to the State apparatus, to be outside its sovereignty and prior to its law: it comes from elsewhere. Indra, the warrior god, is in opposition to Varuna no less than to Mitra.³ He can no more be reduced to one or the other than he can constitute a third of their kind. Rather, he is like a pure and immeasurable multiplicity, the pack, an irruption of the ephemeral and the power of metamorphosis. He unties the bond just as he betrays the pact. He brings a furor to bear against sovereignty, a celerity against gravity, secrecy against the public, a power (puissance) against sovereignty, a machine against the apparatus. He bears witness to another kind of justice, one of incomprehensible cruelty at times, | Either the State disposes of a violence that does not pass through war: it employs police officers and jailers rather than warriors, it has no arms and has no need of them, it acts by immediate magical *capture*, it « ***saisit*** <span style="color:green">[seizes – law: saisie, the seizure of goods by court order; the bailiff's verb]</span> » and « *lie* », preventing all combat. Or else the State acquires an army, but one that presupposes a juridical integration of war and the organization of a military function.² As for the *machine de guerre* in itself, it seems indeed irreducible to the *appareil d'Etat*, exterior to its sovereignty, ***préalable à son droit*** <span style="color:green">[prior to its right – droit: law as a body of right, not loi, statute; préalable, the precondition a court rules on first; Massumi: "prior to its law"]</span>: it comes from elsewhere. Indra, the warrior god, opposes himself no less to Varuna than to Mitra.³ He does not reduce to one of the two, any more than he forms a third. He would rather be like pure multiplicity ***sans mesure*** <span style="color:green">[without measure – unmeasured and immoderate at once: the first appearance of measure, which the numbering number of 12¶ Proposition VI will take up]</span>, the ***meute*** <span style="color:green">[pack – hunting register: the pack of hounds or wolves; the war band as animal multiplicity]</span>, irruption of the ephemeral and ***puissance*** <span style="color:green">[power – puissance: capacity, potency, as against pouvoir, power exercised over others; Massumi marks it with "(puissance)" only later in the sentence]</span> of metamorphosis. He ***dénoue*** <span style="color:green">[unknots – dé-nouer, the knot undone: the bind-chain worked in reverse, cf. lieur 12¶1]</span> the *lien* as much as he betrays the pact. He ***fait valoir*** <span style="color:green">[brings to bear – faire valoir: to assert a claim, to make something count]</span> a ***furor*** <span style="color:green">[Latin: the warrior's frenzy, Dumézil's term for the rage of Indra and the berserker]</span> against ***la mesure*** <span style="color:green">[measure – Massumi: "against sovereignty", which repeats the last term of the series and loses measure, set against the war machine as « sans mesure » two sentences earlier]</span>, a celerity against ***gravité*** <span style="color:green">[gravity – the weight of grave (12¶1), now the physics of falling bodies against celerity]</span>, a secret against the public, a *puissance* against sovereignty, a machine against the *appareil*. He ***témoigne*** <span style="color:green">[bears witness – témoigner: the register of testimony, cf. attestée 12¶1]</span> for another justice, sometimes of an incomprehensible cruelty, but sometimes<br>Notes: ¹ Georges Dumézil, <u>Mitra-Varuna</u>, Gallimard (on the *nexum* and the *mutuum*, the *lien* and the contract, cf. 118-124). [English: <u>Mitra-Varuna</u>, trans. Derek Coltman, Zone, 1988.] ² The State, following its first pole (Varuna, Ouranos, Romulus), operates by magic *lien*, *prise* or immediate *capture*: it does not fight, and has no *machine de guerre*, « it binds, and that is all ». Following its other pole (Mitra, Zeus, Numa), it appropriates an army, but by submitting it to institutional and juridical rules that make of it no more than a piece of the *appareil d'Etat*: thus Mars-Tiwaz is not a warrior god, but a god « jurist of war ». Cf. [note continues in 12¶3] |
| 12¶3 fr.434 umn.352-53 | Dumézil, Mitra-Varuna, pp. 113 sq., 148 sq., 202 sq. 3. Dumézil, Heur et malheur du guerrier, P. U. F. aussi d'une pitié inconnue (puisqu'il dénoue les liens 4). Il témoigne surtout d'autres rapports avec les femmes, avec les animaux, puisqu'il vit toute chose dans des rapports de devenir, au lieu d'opérer des répartitions binaires entre « états » : tout un devenir-animal du guerrier, tout un devenir-femme, qui outrepasse aussi bien les dualités de termes que les correspondances de rapports. A tout égard, la machine de guerre est d'une autre espèce, d'une autre nature, d'une autre origine que l'appareil d'Etat. Il faudrait prendre un exemple limité, comparer la machine de guerre et l'appareil d'Etat suivant la théorie des jeux. Soit les Echecs et le Go, du point de vue des pièces, des rapports entre les pièces et de l'espace concerné. Les échecs sont un jeu d'Etat, ou de cour, l'empereur de Chine y joue. Les pièces d'échecs sont codées, elles ont une nature intérieure ou des propriétés intrinsèques, d'où découlent leurs mouvements, leurs situations, leurs affrontements. Elles sont qualifiées, le cavalier reste un cavalier, le fantassin un fantassin, le voltigeur un voltigeur. Chacune est comme un sujet d'énoncé, doué d'un pouvoir relatif ; et ces pouvoirs relatifs se combinent dans un sujet d'énonciation, le joueur d'échecs lui-même ou la forme d'intériorité du jeu. Les pions de go au contraire sont des grains, des pastilles, de simples unités arithmétiques, et n'ont d'autre fonction qu'anonyme, collective ou de troisième personne : « Il » avance, ce peut être un homme, une femme, une puce, un éléphant. Les pions de go sont les éléments d'un agencement machinique non subjectivé, sans propriétés intrinsèques, mais seulement de situation. | but at others of unequaled pity as well (because he unties bonds...).⁴ He bears witness, above all, to other relations with women, with animals, because he sees all things in relations of becoming, rather than implementing binary distributions between "states": a veritable becoming-animal of the warrior, a becoming-woman, which lies outside dualities of terms as well as correspondences between relations. In every respect, the war machine is of another species, another nature, another origin than the State apparatus. Let us take a limited example and compare the war machine and the State apparatus in the context of the theory of games. Let us take chess and Go, from the standpoint of the game pieces, the relations between the pieces and the space involved. Chess is a game of State, or of the court: the emperor of China played it. Chess pieces are coded; they have an internal nature and intrinsic properties from which their movements, situations, and confrontations derive. They have qualities; a knight remains a knight, a pawn a pawn, a bishop a bishop. Each is like a subject of the statement endowed with a relative power, and these relative powers combine in a subject of enunciation, that is, the chess player or the game's form of interiority. Go pieces, in contrast, are pellets, disks, simple arithmetic units, and have only an anonymous, collective, or third-person function: "It" makes a move. "It" could be a man, a woman, a louse, an elephant. Go pieces are elements of a nonsubjectified machine assemblage with no intrinsic properties, only situational ones. | also of an unknown pity (since he *dénoue* the *liens*).⁴ He bears witness above all to other relations with women, with animals, since he ***vit*** <span style="color:green">[lives – vivre, present tense: he lives all things in becomings; Massumi: "sees", which makes becoming a way of looking]</span> every thing in relations of ***devenir*** <span style="color:green">[becoming – the concept of plateau 10, here the warrior's mode of relation]</span>, instead of ***opérer*** <span style="color:green">[operating – opération: surgical and military register, an action done to a body]</span> binary ***répartitions*** <span style="color:green">[distributions, allotments – répartir, to share out: the vocabulary of distribution that the nomos of Proposition V will take up]</span> between « ***états*** <span style="color:green">[states – lowercase: fixed conditions, punning on the État; capitalization is load-bearing]</span> »: a whole becoming-animal of the warrior, a whole becoming-woman, which ***outrepasse*** <span style="color:green">[oversteps – outre-passer: to go past a bound, as one oversteps a right; Massumi: "lies outside", which makes the movement a position]</span> the dualities of terms as much as the correspondences of relations. In every respect, the *machine de guerre* is of another species, of another nature, of another origin than the *appareil d'Etat*. ***Il faudrait*** <span style="color:green">[one would have to – conditional of necessity; Massumi: "Let us take", an imperative]</span> take a limited example, compare the *machine de guerre* and the *appareil d'Etat* according to the theory of games. ***Soit*** <span style="color:green">[let there be – the mathematician's posit, as in soit x: the treatise sets up its case]</span> Chess and Go, from the point of view of the pieces, of the relations between the pieces, and of the space concerned. Chess is a game of State, or of the court: the emperor of China plays it. The pieces of chess are ***codées*** <span style="color:green">[coded – the code of plateau 3 and Anti-Oedipus: a rule fixing the quality of elements]</span>, they have an inner nature or intrinsic properties, from which flow their movements, their situations, their confrontations. They are ***qualifiées*** <span style="color:green">[qualified – given a quality and a rank, as a worker is qualified; Massumi: "They have qualities", which loses the assignment]</span>, the ***cavalier*** <span style="color:green">[horseman, knight – the chess knight, but also cavalry]</span> remains a *cavalier*, the ***fantassin*** <span style="color:green">[foot soldier – D&G name the pieces as troops; Massumi: "pawn"]</span> a *fantassin*, the ***voltigeur*** <span style="color:green">[skirmisher, light infantryman – also an acrobat or trick rider; Massumi: "bishop", a piece D&G do not name]</span> a *voltigeur*. Each is like a ***sujet d'énoncé*** <span style="color:green">[subject of the statement – linguistics: the subject inside what is said, against the sujet d'énonciation who says it (Benveniste)]</span>, endowed with a relative ***pouvoir*** <span style="color:green">[power – pouvoir: power exercised, rank in relation, against the puissance of 12¶2]</span>; and these relative *pouvoirs* combine in a ***sujet d'énonciation*** <span style="color:green">[subject of enunciation – the one who speaks the statement; here the player]</span>, the chess player himself or the form of interiority of the game. The pieces of Go, on the contrary, are ***grains*** <span style="color:green">[grains – seeds, beads: small counted units, cf. the seeds of plateau 13's stock]</span>, ***pastilles*** <span style="color:green">[lozenges, pellets – small discs; Massumi: "disks"]</span>, simple arithmetic unities, and have no function other than anonymous, collective, or of the third person: « ***Il*** <span style="color:green">[He, It – the French third person is both the impersonal it and he; Massumi: "It"]</span> » advances, it can be a man, a woman, a ***puce*** <span style="color:green">[flea – Massumi: "louse"]</span>, an elephant. The pieces of Go are the elements of an ***agencement*** <span style="color:green">[arranging, fitting-together – everyday register: the layout or fitting-out of a space; Massumi's "assemblage", a rendering the reception still disputes]</span> machinic, nonsubjectified, without intrinsic properties, but only properties of situation.<br>Notes: ² [continued from 12¶2] … Dumézil, <u>Mitra-Varuna</u>, pp. 113 ff., 148 ff., 202 ff. ³ Dumézil, <u>Heur et malheur du guerrier</u>, P.U.F. [English: <u>The Destiny of the Warrior</u>, trans. Alf Hiltebeitel, Chicago, 1970.] |
| 12¶4 fr.434 umn.353 | Aussi les rapports sont-ils très différents dans les deux cas. Dans leur milieu d'intériorité, les pièces d'échecs entretiennent des rapports bi-univoques les unes avec les autres, et avec celles de l'adversaire : leurs fonctions sont structurales. Tandis qu'un pion de go n'a qu'un milieu d'extériorité, ou des rapports extrinsèques avec des nébuleuses, des constellations, d'après lesquels il remplit des fonctions d'insertion ou de situation, comme border, encercler, faire éclater. A lui tout seul, un pion de go peut annihiler synchroniquement toute une constellation, tandis qu'une pièce d'échecs ne le peut pas (ou ne le peut que diachroniquement). Les échecs sont bien une guerre, mais une guerre institutionnalisée, réglée, codée, avec un front, des arrières, des batailles. Mais une guerre sans ligne de combat, sans affrontement et arrières, à la limite sans bataille, c'est le propre du go : pure stratégie, tandis | Thus the relations are very different in the two cases. Within their milieu of interiority, chess pieces entertain biunivocal relations with one another, and with the adversary's pieces: their functioning is structural. On the other hand, a Go piece has only a milieu of exteriority, or extrinsic relations with nebulas or constellations, according to which it fulfills functions of insertion or situation, such as bordering, encircling, shattering. All by itself, a Go piece can destroy an entire constellation synchronically; a chess piece cannot (or can do so diachronically only). Chess is indeed a war, but an institutionalized, regulated, coded war, with a front, a rear, battles. But what is proper to Go is war without battle lines, with neither confrontation nor retreat, without battles even: pure strategy, whereas | So the relations are very different in the two cases. In their *milieu d'intériorité*, the pieces of chess maintain ***bi-univoques*** <span style="color:green">[one-to-one – mathematics: a bijective correspondence, each term paired with exactly one other]</span> relations with one another, and with those of the adversary: their functions are structural. Whereas a piece of Go has only a ***milieu d'extériorité*** <span style="color:green">[milieu of exteriority – the open outside, against the milieu d'intériorité of 12¶1]</span>, or extrinsic relations with ***nébuleuses*** <span style="color:green">[nebulae – astronomy: diffuse clouds of stars; also the vague, the not-yet-formed]</span>, constellations, according to which it fulfills functions of insertion or of situation, such as ***border*** <span style="color:green">[to border, to edge – also to hem a garment or to line a road: to occupy an edge]</span>, encircling, ***faire éclater*** <span style="color:green">[to make burst – éclater: to explode, to shatter into fragments; Massumi: "shattering"]</span>. All on its own, a piece of Go can ***annihiler*** <span style="color:green">[annihilate – reduce to nothing; Massumi: "destroy"]</span> a whole constellation synchronically, whereas a piece of chess cannot (or can only diachronically). Chess is indeed a war, but an institutionalized war, *réglée*, *codée*, with a front, ***arrières*** <span style="color:green">[rear lines – the rear of an army, its supply and reserve; Massumi has "retreat" in the next sentence, where the French again says arrières]</span>, battles. But a war without battle line, without confrontation and *arrières*, ***à la limite*** <span style="color:green">[at the limit – the limit-case, pushed to the extreme point; Massumi: "even", which drops the limit]</span> without battle, this is ***le propre*** <span style="color:green">[the proper – what belongs to a thing as its own, its distinguishing property]</span> of Go: pure strategy, whereas |
| 12¶5 fr.436 umn.353 | 4. Sur le rôle du guerrier, en tant qu'il « délie » et s'oppose au lien magique autant qu'au contrat juridique, cf. Mitra-Varuna, pp. 124-132. Et, passim chez Dumézil, l'analyse de la furor. que les échecs sont une sémiologie. Enfin, ce n'est pas du tout le même espace : dans le cas des échecs, il s'agit de se distribuer un espace fermé, donc d'aller d'un point à un autre, d'occuper un maximum de places avec un minimum de pièces. Dans le go, il s'agit de se distribuer dans un espace ouvert, de tenir l'espace, de garder la possibilité de surgir en n'importe quel point : le mouvement ne va plus d'un point à un autre, mais devient perpétuel, sans but ni destination, sans départ ni arrivée. Espace « lisse » du go, contre espace « strié » des échecs. Nomos du go contre Etat des échecs, nomos contre polis. C'est que les échecs codent et décodent l'espace, tandis que le go procède tout autrement, le territorialise et le déterritorialise (faire du dehors un territoire dans l'espace, consolider ce territoire par construction d'un second territoire adjacent, déterritorialiser l'ennemi par éclatement interne de son territoire, se déterritorialiser soi-même en renonçant, en allant ailleurs...). Une autre justice, un autre mouvement, un autre espace-temps. « Ils arrivent comme la destinée, sans cause, sans raison, sans égard, sans prétexte... » | chess is a semiology. Finally, the space is not at all the same: in chess, it is a question of arranging a closed space for oneself, thus of going from one point to another, of occupying the maximum number of squares with the minimum number of pieces. In Go, it is a question of arraying oneself in an open space, of holding space, of maintaining the possibility of springing up at any point: the movement is not from one point to another, but becomes perpetual, without aim or destination, without departure or arrival. The "smooth" space of Go, as against the "striated" space of chess. The nomos of Go against the State of chess, nomos against polis. The difference is that chess codes and decodes space, whereas Go proceeds altogether differently, territorializing or deterritorializing it (make the outside a territory in space; consolidate that territory by the construction of a second, adjacent territory; deterritorialize the enemy by shattering his territory from within; deterritorialize oneself by renouncing, by going elsewhere...). Another justice, another movement, another space-time. "They come like fate, without reason, consideration, or pretext | that chess is a semiology. Finally, it is not at all the same space: in the case of chess, it is a matter of ***se distribuer un espace*** <span style="color:green">[allotting oneself a space – distributing the space itself, cutting it into shares; Massumi: "arranging a closed space for oneself"]</span> closed, thus of going from one point to another, of occupying a maximum of places with a minimum of pieces. In Go, it is a matter of ***se distribuer dans un espace*** <span style="color:green">[distributing oneself within a space – the same verb with the preposition changed: spreading out in space rather than dividing it, the nomos distinction in a word; Massumi: "arraying oneself", which hides the contrast]</span> open, of ***tenir*** <span style="color:green">[holding – tenir l'espace: to hold, keep, occupy the space, as troops hold ground]</span> the space, of keeping the possibility of ***surgir*** <span style="color:green">[surging up – sudden appearance at any point, cf. the State that surgit d'un coup]</span> at any point whatever: the movement no longer goes from one point to another, but becomes perpetual, without aim or destination, without departure or arrival. Space « ***lisse*** <span style="color:green">[smooth – from Boulez's temps lisse, time occupied without being counted, against temps strié or pulsé]</span> » of Go, against space « ***strié*** <span style="color:green">[striated – grooved, scored with parallel lines: Boulez's counted, pulsed time]</span> » of chess. ***Nomos*** <span style="color:green">[Greek: distribution, pasture, then law; from nemein, to share out (Laroche, note 51 of the plateau)]</span> of Go against State of chess, *nomos* against polis. It is that chess codes and decodes space, whereas Go proceeds altogether otherwise, ***le territorialise et le déterritorialise*** <span style="color:green">[territorializes it and deterritorializes it – both at once; Massumi: "territorializing or deterritorializing"]</span> (to make of the outside a territory in space, to consolidate this territory by the construction of a second, adjacent territory, to deterritorialize the enemy by internal bursting of his territory, to deterritorialize oneself by renouncing, by going elsewhere…). Another justice, another movement, another space-time. « They arrive like destiny, without cause, without reason, without regard, without pretext… »<br>Notes: ⁴ On the role of the warrior, insofar as he « unbinds » and opposes himself to the magic *lien* as much as to the juridical contract, cf. <u>Mitra-Varuna</u>, pp. 124-132. And, passim in Dumézil, the analysis of the *furor*. |
| 12¶6 fr.436 umn.353-54 | « Impossible de comprendre comment ils ont pénétré jusqu'à la capitale, cependant ils sont là, et chaque matin semble accroître leur nombre... » - Luc de Heusch a dégagé un mythe bantou qui nous renvoie au même schéma : Nkongolo, empereur autochtone, organisateur de grands travaux, homme de public et de police, donne ses demi-sœurs au chasseur Mbidi, qui l'aide, puis s'en va ; le fils de Mbidi, l'homme du secret, rejoint son père, mais pour revenir du dehors, avec cette chose inimaginable, une armée, et tuer Nkongolo, quitte à refaire un nouvel Etat 5. « Entre » l'Etat despotique-magique et l'Etat juridique qui comprend une institution militaire, il y aurait cette fulguration de la machine de guerre, venue du dehors. Du point de vue de l'Etat, l'originalité de l'homme de guerre, son excentricité, apparaît nécessairement sous une forme négative : bêtise, difformité, folie, illégitimité, usurpation, péché... Dumézil analyse les trois « péchés » du guerrier dans la tradition indo-européenne : contre le roi, contre le prêtre, contre les lois qui découlent de l'Etat (soit une transgression sexuelle qui compromet la répartition des hommes et des femmes, soit même une trahison des lois de la guerre telles qu'elles sont instituées [...] 5. Luc de Heusch (Le roi ivre ou l'origine de l'Etat) insiste sur le caractère public des gestes de Nkongolo, par opposition au secret des gestes de Mbidi et de son fils : l'un notamment mange en public, tandis que les autres se dissimulent pendant les repas. Nous verrons le rapport essentiel du secret avec une machine de guerre, tant du point de vue du principe que des conséquences : espionnage, stratégie, diplomatie. Les commentateurs ont souvent souligné ce rapport. [...] par l'Etat 6). | In some way that is incomprehensible they have pushed right into the capital. At any rate, here they are; it seems that every morning there are more of them."⁵ Luc de Heusch analyzes a Bantu myth that leads us to the same schema: Nkongolo, an indigenous emperor and administrator of public works, a man of the public and a man of the police, gives his half-sisters to the hunter Mbidi, who assists him and then leaves. Mbidi's son, a man of secrecy, joins up with his father, only to return from the outside with that inconceivable thing, an army. He kills Nkongolo and proceeds to build a new State.⁶ "Between" the magical-despotic State and the juridical State containing a military institution, we see the flash of the war machine, arriving from without. From the standpoint of the State, the originality of the man of war, his eccentricity, necessarily appears in a negative form: stupidity, deformity, madness, illegitimacy, usurpation, sin. Dumezil analyzes the three "sins" of the warrior in the Indo-European tradition: against the king, against the priest, against the laws originating in the State (for example, a sexual transgression that compromises the distribution of men and women, or even a betrayal of the laws of war as instituted by the State).⁷ | « Impossible to understand how they have penetrated as far as the capital, yet they are there, and each morning seems to increase their number… » – Luc de Heusch has ***dégagé*** <span style="color:green">[drawn out, disengaged – to free a structure from its material, as one clears a site; Massumi: "analyzes"]</span> a Bantu myth that sends us back to the same schema: Nkongolo, autochthonous emperor, organizer of ***grands travaux*** <span style="color:green">[great works – the set phrase for State public works, monuments, and corvées]</span>, man of public and of police, gives his half-sisters to the hunter Mbidi, who helps him, then goes away; the son of Mbidi, the man of the secret, rejoins his father, but to come back from ***dehors*** <span style="color:green">[outside – the dehors of Blanchot and Foucault, the outside as a form, not a place]</span>, with this unimaginable thing, an army, and kill Nkongolo, ***quitte à*** <span style="color:green">[even if it means, at the cost of – the concessive: refounding a State is the price of the act, not its aim; Massumi: "proceeds to build"]</span> remake a new State.⁵ « Between » the despotic-magical State and the juridical State that comprises a military institution, there ***il y aurait*** <span style="color:green">[there would be – conditional of hypothesis; Massumi: "we see", which turns the hypothesis into an observation]</span> this ***fulguration*** <span style="color:green">[lightning-flash – fulgur, lightning; also the theological fulguration by which a world flashes forth; Massumi: "flash"]</span> of the *machine de guerre*, come from *dehors*. From the point of view of the State, the originality of the man of war, his ***excentricité*** <span style="color:green">[eccentricity – literally off-center, outside the center that the State occupies]</span>, necessarily appears under a negative form: ***bêtise*** <span style="color:green">[stupidity – the bêtise of Difference and Repetition, the stupidity proper to thought]</span>, deformity, madness, illegitimacy, usurpation, sin… Dumézil analyzes the three « sins » of the warrior in the Indo-European tradition: against the king, against the priest, against the laws that flow from the State (whether a sexual transgression that compromises the *répartition* of men and women, or even a betrayal of the laws of war such as they are instituted by the State⁶).<br>Notes: ⁵ [Minn: 6, Fr: 5] Luc de Heusch (<u>Le roi ivre ou l'origine de l'Etat</u>) insists on the public character of the gestures of Nkongolo, as opposed to the secret of the gestures of Mbidi and his son: the one notably eats in public, while the others hide themselves during meals. We will see the essential relation of the secret with a *machine de guerre*, as much from the point of view of principle as of consequences: espionage, strategy, diplomacy. Commentators have often underlined this relation. [<u>The Drunken King, or The Origin of the State</u>, trans. Roy Willis, Indiana, 1982.] |
| 12¶7 fr.436 umn.354 | Le guerrier est dans la situation de tout trahir, y compris la fonction militaire, ou de ne rien comprendre. Il arrive à des historiens, bourgeois ou soviétiques, de suivre cette tradition négative, et d'expliquer que Gengis Khan ne comprend rien : il « ne comprend pas » le phénomène étatique, il « ne comprend pas » le phénomène urbain. Facile à dire. C'est que l'extériorité de la machine de guerre par rapport à l'appareil d'Etat se révèle partout, mais reste difficile à penser. Il ne suffit pas d'affirmer que la machine est extérieure à l'appareil, il faut arriver à penser la machine de guerre comme étant elle-même une pure forme d'extériorité, tandis que l'appareil d'Etat constitue la forme d'intériorité que nous prenons habituellement pour modèle, ou d'après laquelle nous avons l'habitude de penser. Ce qui complique tout, c'est que cette puissance extrinsèque de la machine de guerre tend, dans certaines circonstances, à se confondre elle-même avec l'une ou l'autre des têtes de l'appareil d'Etat. Tantôt elle se confond avec la violence magique d'Etat, tantôt avec l'institution militaire d'Etat. Par exemple, la machine de guerre invente la vitesse et le secret ; mais il y a pourtant une certaine vitesse et un certain secret qui appartiennent à l'Etat, relativement, secondairement. Il y a donc un grand risque d'identifier le rapport structural entre les deux pôles de la souveraineté politique, et le rapport dynamique de ces deux pôles ensemble avec la puissance de guerre. | The warrior is in the position of betraying everything, including the function of the military, or of understanding nothing. It happens that historians, both bourgeois and Soviet, will follow this negative tradition and explain how Genghis Khan understood nothing: he "didn't understand" the phenomenon of the city. An easy thing to say. The problem is that the exteriority of the war machine in relation to the State apparatus is everywhere apparent but remains difficult to conceptualize. It is not enough to affirm that the war machine is external to the apparatus. It is necessary to reach the point of conceiving the war machine as itself a pure form of exteriority, whereas the State apparatus constitutes the form of interiority we habitually take as a model, or according to which we are in the habit of thinking. What complicates everything is that this extrinsic power of the war machine tends, under certain circumstances, to become confused with one of the two heads of the State apparatus. Sometimes it is confused with the magic violence of the State, at other times with the State's military institution. For instance, the war machine invents speed and secrecy; but there is all the same a certain speed and a certain secrecy that pertain to the State, relatively, secondarily. So there is a great danger of identifying the structural relation between the two poles of political sovereignty, and the dynamic interrelation of these two poles, with the power of war. | The warrior is in the situation of betraying everything, including the military function, or of understanding nothing. It happens to historians, bourgeois or Soviet, to follow this negative tradition, and to explain that Genghis Khan understands nothing: he « does not understand » ***le phénomène étatique*** <span style="color:green">[the State phenomenon – Massumi omits this clause and keeps only the city]</span>, he « does not understand » the urban phenomenon. Easy to say. It is that the exteriority of the *machine de guerre* in relation to the *appareil d'Etat* reveals itself everywhere, but remains difficult ***à penser*** <span style="color:green">[to think – penser, not concevoir; Massumi: "to conceptualize", which makes it a matter of concepts rather than thought]</span>. It is not enough to affirm that the machine is exterior to the *appareil*, one must arrive at thinking the *machine de guerre* as being itself a pure form of exteriority, whereas the *appareil d'Etat* constitutes the form of interiority that we habitually take for a model, or according to which we have the habit of thinking. What complicates everything is that this extrinsic *puissance* of the *machine de guerre* tends, in certain circumstances, ***à se confondre*** <span style="color:green">[to confuse itself, to merge – se confondre: to be mistaken for, and to melt into; the confusion is a fusion]</span> itself with one or the other of the heads of the *appareil d'Etat*. Now it *se confond* with the magic violence of State, now with the military institution of State. For example, the *machine de guerre* invents ***la vitesse et le secret*** <span style="color:green">[speed and the secret – the two traits Kleist's war machine will carry, with affect (12¶ on Kleist)]</span>; but there are nonetheless a certain speed and a certain secret that belong to the State, relatively, secondarily. There is thus a great risk of identifying the structural relation between the two poles of political sovereignty, and the dynamic relation of these two poles together, with the *puissance* of war. |
| 12¶8 fr.436 umn.354 | Dumézil cite la lignée des rois de Rome : le rapport Romulus-Numa qui se reproduit le long d'une série, avec variantes et alternance entre les deux types de souverains également légitimes ; mais aussi le rapport avec un « mauvais roi », Tullus Hostilius, Tarquin le Superbe, l'irruption du guerrier comme personnage inquiétant, illégitime 7. On pourrait invoquer aussi bien les rois de Shakespeare : même la violence, les meurtres et les perversions n'empêchent pas la lignée d'Etat de former de « bons » rois ; mais se glisse un personnage inquiétant, Richard III, qui annonce dès le début son intention de réinventer une machine de guerre et d'en imposer la ligne (difforme, fourbe et traître, il se réclame d'un « but secret » tout autre que la conquête du pouvoir d'Etat, et d'un autre rapport avec les femmes). Bref, chaque fois que l'on confond l'irruption de la puissance de guerre avec la lignée de domination d'Etat, tout se 6. Dumézil, Mythe et épopée, Gallimard, II, pp. 17-19 : analyse des trois péchés, qu'on retrouve dans le cas du dieu indien Indra, du héros scandinave Starcatherus, du héros grec Héraclès. Cf. aussi Heur et malheur du guerrier. 7. Dumézil, Mitra-Varuna, p. 135. Dumézil analyse les risques et les raisons de la confusion, qui peuvent tenir à des variantes économiques, cf. pp. 153, 159. | Dumezil cites the lineage of the Roman kings: there is a Romulus-Numa relation that recurs throughout a series, with variants and an alternation between these two types of equally legitimate rulers; but there is also a relation with an "evil king," Tullus Hostilius, Tarquinius Superbus, an upsurge of the warrior as a disquieting and illegitimate character.⁸ Shakespeare's kings could also be invoked: even violence, murders, and perversion do not prevent the State lineage from producing "good" kings; but a disturbing character like Richard III slips in, announcing from the outset his intention to reinvent a war machine and impose its line (deformed, treacherous and traitorous, he claims a "secret close intent"⁹ totally different from the conquest of State power, and another -an other-relation with women). In short, whenever the irruption of war power is confused with the line of State domination, everything | Dumézil cites the ***lignée*** <span style="color:green">[lineage, line of descent – lignée, the dynastic line; set against la ligne the warrior imposes below]</span> of the kings of Rome: the Romulus-Numa relation that reproduces itself along a series, with variants and alternation between the two types of equally legitimate sovereigns; but also the relation with a « bad king », Tullus Hostilius, Tarquin the Proud, the irruption of the warrior as a disquieting, illegitimate character.⁷ One could just as well invoke the kings of Shakespeare: even violence, murders, and perversions do not prevent the *lignée* of State from forming « good » kings; but there ***se glisse*** <span style="color:green">[slips in – se glisser: to slide in unnoticed, as through a gap]</span> a disquieting character, Richard III, who announces from the start his intention to reinvent a *machine de guerre* and to impose its ***ligne*** <span style="color:green">[line – the war machine's line, against the State's lignée; the line of flight to come]</span> (deformed, ***fourbe*** <span style="color:green">[crafty, double-dealing – the knave of comedy]</span> and traitor, he ***se réclame*** <span style="color:green">[lays claim to, invokes as his warrant]</span> a « secret aim » altogether other than the conquest of State *pouvoir*, and another relation with women). In short, each time one confuses the irruption of the *puissance* of war with the *lignée* of State domination, everything<br>Notes: ⁶ [Minn: 7, Fr: 6] Dumézil, <u>Mythe et épopée</u>, Gallimard, II, pp. 17-19: analysis of the three sins, which one finds again in the case of the Indian god Indra, of the Scandinavian hero Starcatherus, of the Greek hero Heracles. Cf. also <u>Heur et malheur du guerrier</u>. [No English translation of <u>Mythe et épopée</u> II; for the second, <u>The Destiny of the Warrior</u>.] ⁷ [Minn: 8, Fr: 7] Dumézil, <u>Mitra-Varuna</u>, p. 135. Dumézil analyzes the risks and the reasons of the confusion, which can stem from economic variants, cf. pp. 153, 159. |
| 12¶9 fr.439 umn.354-55 | brouille, et l'on ne peut plus comprendre la machine de guerre que sous les espèces du négatif, puisqu'on ne laisse rien subsister d'extérieur à l'Etat lui-même. Mais, replacée dans son milieu d'extériorité, la machine de guerre apparaît d'une autre espèce, d'une autre nature, d'une autre origine. On dirait qu'elle s'installe entre les deux têtes de l'Etat, entre les deux articulations, et qu'elle est nécessaire pour passer de l'une à l'autre. Mais justement, « entre » les deux, elle affirme dans l'instant, même éphémère, même fulgurant, son irréductibilité. L'Etat n'a pas par lui-même de machine de guerre ; il se l'appropriera seulement sous forme d'institution militaire, et celle-ci ne cessera pas de lui poser des problèmes. D'où la méfiance des Etats vis-à-vis de leur institution militaire, en tant qu'elle hérite d'une machine de guerre extrinsèque. Clausewitz a le pressentiment de cette situation générale, lorsqu'il traite le flux de guerre absolue comme une Idée, que les Etats s'approprient partiellement suivant les besoins de leur politique, et par rapport à laquelle ils sont plus ou moins bons « conducteurs ». Coincé entre les deux pôles de la souveraineté politique, l'homme de guerre apparaît dépassé, condamné, sans avenir, réduit à sa propre fureur qu'il tourne contre lui-même. Les descendants d'Héraclès, Achille, puis Ajax, ont encore assez de forces pour affirmer leur indépendance à l'égard d'Agamemnon, l'homme du vieil Etat, mais ils ne peuvent rien contre Ulysse, l'homme d'Etat moderne naissant, le premier homme d'Etat moderne. Et c'est Ulysse qui hérite des armes d'Achille, pour en changer l'usage, les soumettre au droit d'Etat, ce n'est pas Ajax, condamné par la déesse qu'il a bravée, contre laquelle il a péché 8. | gets muddled; the war machine can then be understood only through the categories of the negative, since nothing is left that remains outside the State. But, returned to its milieu of exteriority, the war machine is seen to be of another species, of another nature, of another origin. One would have to say that it is located between the two heads of the State, between the two articulations, and that it is necessary in order to pass from one to the other. But "between" the two, in that instant, even ephemeral, if only a flash, it proclaims its own irreducibility. The State has no war machine of its own; it can only appropriate one in the form of a military institution, one that will continually cause it problems. This explains the mistrust States have toward their military institutions, in that the military institution inherits an extrinsic war machine. Karl von Clausewitz has a general sense of this situation when he treats the flow of absolute war as an Idea that States partially appropriate according to their political needs, and in relation to which they are more or less good "conductors." Trapped between the two poles of political sovereignty, the man of war seems outmoded, condemned, without a future, reduced to his own fury, which he turns against himself. The descendants of Hercules, Achilles, then Ajax, have enough strength left to proclaim their independence from Agamemnon, a man of the old State. But they are powerless when it comes to Ulysses, a man of the nascent modern State, the first man of the modern State. And it is Ulysses who inherits Achilles' arms, only to convert them to other uses, submitting them to the laws of the State - not Ajax, who is condemned by the goddess he defied and against whom he sinned. | is muddled, and one can no longer understand the *machine de guerre* except ***sous les espèces du négatif*** <span style="color:green">[under the species of the negative – sous les espèces de: in the guise of, as the bread is the species of the host; Massumi: "through the categories of the negative"]</span>, since one lets nothing exterior to the State itself subsist. But, put back in its *milieu d'extériorité*, the *machine de guerre* appears of another species, of another nature, of another origin. ***On dirait*** <span style="color:green">[one would say – it would seem: a tentative description; Massumi: "One would have to say", which adds an obligation]</span> that it installs itself between the two heads of the State, between the two articulations, and that it is necessary in order to pass from the one to the other. But precisely, « between » the two, it affirms in the instant, even ephemeral, even ***fulgurant*** <span style="color:green">[lightning-quick – the adjective of the fulguration of 12¶6]</span>, its irreducibility. The State does not have by itself a *machine de guerre*; it will appropriate one only in the form of a military institution, and this will not cease to pose problems for it. Hence the mistrust of States toward their military institution, insofar as it inherits an extrinsic *machine de guerre*. Clausewitz has the ***pressentiment*** <span style="color:green">[presentiment – a feeling-in-advance of what is not yet there; the anticipation of Proposition XI (431); Massumi: "has a general sense of", which loses it]</span> of this general situation, when he treats the ***flux*** <span style="color:green">[flow, flux – the flux of Anti-Oedipus: what passes, is coded, or escapes]</span> of absolute war as an Idea, which States appropriate partially according to the needs of their politics, and in relation to which they are more or less good « ***conducteurs*** <span style="color:green">[conductors – physics: bodies through which a current passes; also drivers, leaders]</span> ». ***Coincé*** <span style="color:green">[wedged, stuck – caught between two jaws]</span> between the two poles of political sovereignty, the man of war appears surpassed, condemned, without a future, reduced to his own ***fureur*** <span style="color:green">[fury – the French form of the Latin furor of 12¶2]</span>, which he turns against himself. The descendants of Heracles, Achilles, then Ajax, still have enough strength to affirm their independence with regard to Agamemnon, the man of the old State, but they can do nothing against Ulysses, the ***homme d'Etat*** <span style="color:green">[statesman – literally man of State; Massumi keeps the literal "man of the nascent modern State"]</span> of the nascent modern State, the first modern *homme d'Etat*. And it is Ulysses who inherits the arms of Achilles, to change their use, to submit them to the *droit* of State; it is not Ajax, condemned by the goddess whom he braved, against whom he sinned.⁸ |
| 12¶10 fr.439 umn.355-56 | Nul mieux que Kleist n'a montré cette situation de l'homme de guerre, à la fois excentrique et condamné. Car, dans Penthesilée, Achille est déjà séparé de sa puissance : la machine de guerre est passée du côté des Amazones, peuple-femme sans Etat, dont la justice, la religion, les amours sont organisées sur un mode uniquement guerrier. Descendantes des Scythes, les Amazones surgissent comme la foudre, « entre » les deux Etats, le grec et le troyen. Elles balaient tout sur leur passage. Achille se trouve devant son double, Penthesilée. Et dans sa lutte ambiguë, Achille ne peut s'empêcher d'épouser la machine de guerre ou d'aimer Penthesilée, donc de trahir à la fois Agamemnon et Ulysse. Et pourtant, il appartient déjà suffisamment à l'Etat grec pour que Penthesilée de son côté ne puisse entrer avec lui dans le rapport passionnel de la guerre 8. Sur Ajax et la tragédie de Sophocle, cf. l'analyse de Jean Starobinski, Trois fureurs, Gallimard. Starobinski pose explicitement le problème de la guerre et de l'Etat. [...] sans trahir elle-même la loi collective de son peuple, cette loi de meute qui interdit de « choisir » l'ennemi, et d'entrer dans des face-à-face ou des distinctions binaires. C'est dans toute son œuvre que Kleist chante une machine de guerre, et l'oppose à l'appareil d'Etat dans un combat d'avance perdu. Sans doute Arminius annonce-t-il une machine de guerre germanique qui rompt avec l'ordre impérial des alliances et des armées, et se dresse à jamais contre l'Etat romain. Mais le prince de Hombourg ne vit plus que dans un rêve, et se trouve condamné pour avoir obtenu la victoire en désobéissant à la loi d'Etat. Quant à Kohlhaas, sa machine de guerre ne peut plus être que de brigandage. | No one has portrayed the situation of the man of war, at once eccentric and condemned, better than Kleist. In Penthesilea, Achilles is already separated from his power: the war machine has passed over to the Amazons, a Stateless woman-people whose justice, religion, and loves are organized uniquely in a war mode. Descendants of the Scythians, the Amazons spring forth like lightning, "between" the two States, the Greek and the Trojan. They sweep away everything in their path. Achilles is brought before his double, Penthesilea. And in his ambiguous struggle, Achilles is unable to prevent himself from marrying the war machine, or from loving Penthesilea, and thus from betraying Agamemnon and Ulysses at the same time. Nevertheless, he already belongs enough to the Greek State that Penthesilea, for her part, cannot enter the passional relation of war with him without herself betraying the collective law of her people, the law of the pack that prohibits "choosing" the enemy and entering into one-to-one relationships or binary distinctions. Throughout his work, Kleist celebrates the war machine, setting it against the State apparatus in a struggle that is lost from the start. Doubtless Arminius heralds a Germanic war machine that breaks with the imperial order of alliances and armies, and stands forever opposed to the Roman State. But the Prince of Homburg lives only in a dream and stands condemned for having reached victory in disobedience of the law of the State. As for Kohlhaas, his war machine can no longer be anything more than banditry. | No one better than Kleist has shown this situation of the man of war, at once eccentric and condemned. For, in Penthesilea, Achilles is already separated from his *puissance*: the *machine de guerre* has passed to the side of the Amazons, a woman-people without State, whose justice, religion, loves are organized in a uniquely warrior mode. Descendants of the Scythians, the Amazons *surgissent* like lightning, « between » the two States, the Greek and the Trojan. They sweep away everything in their passage. Achilles finds himself before his double, Penthesilea. And in his ambiguous struggle, Achilles cannot keep himself from ***épouser*** <span style="color:green">[marrying – also espousing, taking the shape of, as a garment épouse the body; Massumi: "marrying"]</span> the *machine de guerre* or from loving Penthesilea, thus from betraying at once Agamemnon and Ulysses. And yet he already belongs sufficiently to the Greek State that Penthesilea for her part cannot enter with him into the passional relation of war without herself betraying the collective law of her people, this law of *meute* that forbids « choosing » the enemy, and entering into ***face-à-face*** <span style="color:green">[face-offs, face-to-face confrontations – the duel; Massumi: "one-to-one relationships", which loses the confrontation]</span> or binary distinctions. It is in all his work that Kleist ***chante*** <span style="color:green">[sings – the epic register, to sing of arms and men; Massumi: "celebrates"]</span> a *machine de guerre*, and opposes it to the *appareil d'Etat* in a combat lost in advance. Doubtless Arminius announces a Germanic *machine de guerre* that breaks with the imperial order of alliances and armies, and stands forever against the Roman State. But the prince of Homburg no longer lives except in a dream, and finds himself condemned for having obtained victory by disobeying the law of State. As for Kohlhaas, his *machine de guerre* can no longer be anything but ***brigandage*** <span style="color:green">[brigandage, banditry – the brigand, the armed robber of the highways; cf. the bands of Proposition II]</span>.<br>Notes: ⁸ [Minn: 10, Fr: 8] On Ajax and the tragedy of Sophocles, cf. the analysis of Jean Starobinski, <u>Trois fureurs</u>, Gallimard. Starobinski explicitly poses the problem of war and of the State. [No English translation of <u>Trois fureurs</u>.] |
| 12¶11 fr.439 umn.356 | Est-ce le destin d'une telle machine, lorsque l'Etat triomphe, de tomber dans l'alternative : ou bien n'être plus que l'organe militaire et discipliné de l'appareil d'Etat, ou bien se retourner contre elle-même, et devenir une machine de suicide à deux, pour un homme et une femme solitaires ? Goethe et Hegel, penseurs d'Etat, voient un monstre en Kleist, et Kleist a perdu d'avance. Pourquoi cependant la plus étrange modernité est-elle de son côté ? C'est que les éléments de son œuvre sont le secret, la vitesse et l'affect 9. Et le secret n'est plus chez lui un contenu pris dans une forme d'intériorité, au contraire il devient forme, et s'identifie à la forme d'extériorité toujours hors d'elle-même. De même, les sentiments sont arrachés à l'intériorité d'un « sujet » pour être violemment projetés dans un milieu de pure extériorité qui leur communique une vitesse invraisemblable, une force de catapulte : amour ou haine, ce ne sont plus du tout des sentiments, mais des affects. Et ces affects sont autant de devenir-femme, de devenir-animal du guerrier (l'ours, les chiennes). Les affects traversent le corps comme des flèches, ce sont des armes de guerre. Vitesse de déterritorialisation de l'affect. Même les rêves (celui du prince de Hombourg, celui de Penthesilée) sont extériorisés, par un système de relais et de branchements, d'enchaînements extrinsèques qui appartiennent à la machine de guerre. Anneaux brisés. Cet élément de l'extériorité, qui domine tout, que Kleist invente en littérature, qu'il est le premier à inventer, va donner au temps un nouveau rythme, une succession sans fin de catatonies ou d'évanouissements, et de fulgurations ou précipitations. | Is it the destiny of the war machine, when the State triumphs, to be caught in this alternative: either to be nothing more than the disciplined, military organ of the State apparatus, or to turn against itself, to become a double suicide machine for a solitary man and a solitary woman? Goethe and Hegel, State thinkers both, see Kleist as a monster, and Kleist has lost from the start. Why is it, then, that the most uncanny modernity lies with him? It is because the elements of his work are secrecy, speed, and affect.¹¹ And in Kleist the secret is no longer a content held within a form of interiority; rather, it becomes a form, identified with the form of exteriority that is always external to itself. Similarly, feelings become uprooted from the interiority of a "subject," to be projected violently outward into a milieu of pure exteriority that lends them an incredible velocity, a catapulting force: love or hate, they are no longer feelings but affects. And these affects are so many instances of the becoming-woman, the becoming-animal of the warrior (the bear, she-dogs). Affects transpierce the body like arrows, they are weapons of war. The deterritorialization velocity of affect. Even dreams (Homburg's, Pentheselea's) are externalized, by a system of relays and plug-ins, extrinsic linkages belonging to the war machine. Broken rings. This element of exteriority - which dominates everything, which Kleist invents in literature, which he is the first to invent - will give time a new rhythm: an endless succession of catatonic episodes or fainting spells, and flashes or rushes. | Is it the destiny of such a machine, when the State triumphs, to fall into the alternative: either to be no more than the military and disciplined organ of the *appareil d'Etat*, or to turn back against itself, and become a suicide machine for two, for a solitary man and woman? Goethe and Hegel, thinkers of State, see a monster in Kleist, and Kleist has lost in advance. Why, however, is the most ***étrange*** <span style="color:green">[strange – Massumi: "uncanny", which imports Freud's unheimlich]</span> modernity on his side? It is that the elements of his work are the secret, speed, and ***l'affect*** <span style="color:green">[affect – Spinoza's affectus, a passage of power that traverses bodies, against the sentiment, a feeling owned by a subject]</span>.⁹ And the secret is no longer, with him, a content *pris* in a form of interiority; on the contrary it becomes form, and identifies itself with the form of exteriority always outside itself. In the same way, ***les sentiments*** <span style="color:green">[feelings, sentiments – the personal, interior emotion, which D&G will set against affect in Proposition VII]</span> are torn from the interiority of a « subject » to be violently projected into a milieu of pure exteriority that communicates to them an unlikely speed, a force of catapult: love or hate, these are no longer at all *sentiments*, but *affects*. And these *affects* are so many becomings-woman, becomings-animal of the warrior (the bear, the she-dogs). *Affects* traverse the body like arrows, they are weapons of war. Speed of deterritorialization of the *affect*. Even dreams (the prince of Homburg's, Penthesilea's) are exteriorized, by a system of relays and ***branchements*** <span style="color:green">[branchings, hook-ups – electrical connections, plug-ins; also branch lines]</span>, of extrinsic linkings that belong to the *machine de guerre*. Broken rings. This element of exteriority, which dominates everything, which Kleist invents in literature, which he is the first to invent, will give time a new rhythm, an endless succession of catatonias or faintings, and of *fulgurations* or ***précipitations*** <span style="color:green">[precipitations, rushes – haste and headlong fall; chemistry: the precipitate that falls out of a solution]</span>. |
| 12¶12 fr.439 umn.356 | La catatonie, c'est « cet affect est trop fort pour moi », et la fulguration, « la force de cet affect m'emporte », le Moi n'étant plus qu'un personnage dont les gestes et les émotions sont désubjectivés, quitte à en mourir. Telle est la formule personnelle de Kleist : une succession de courses [...] folles et de catatonies figées, où ne subsiste plus aucune intériorité subjective. Il y a beaucoup d'Orient chez Kleist : le lutteur japonais, immobile interminablement, puis qui fait un geste trop rapide pour être perçu. Le joueur de go. Beaucoup de choses dans l'art moderne viennent de Kleist. Goethe et Hegel sont de vieux hommes par rapport à Kleist. Se peut-il qu'au moment où la machine de guerre n'existe plus, vaincu par l'Etat, elle témoigne au plus haut point de son irréductibilité, elle essaime dans des machines à penser, à aimer, à mourir, à créer, qui disposent de forces vives ou révolutionnaires susceptibles de remettre en question l'Etat vainqueur ? C'est dans le même mouvement que la machine de guerre est déjà dépassée, condamnée, appropriée, et qu'elle prend de nouvelles formes, se métamorphose, en affirmant son irréductibilité, son extériorité : déployer ce milieu d'extériorité pure, que l'homme d'Etat occidental, ou le penseur occidental, ne cessent pas de réduire ? | Catatonia is: "This affect is too strong for me," and a flash is: "The power of this affect sweeps me away," so that the Self (Moi) is now nothing more than a character whose actions and emotions are desubjectified, perhaps even to the point of death. Such is Kleist's personal formula: a succession of flights of madness and catatonic freezes in which no subjective interiority remains. There is much of the East in Kleist: the Japanese fighter, interminably still, who then makes a move too quick to see. The Go player. Many things in modern art come from Kleist. Goethe and Hegel are old men next to Kleist. Could it be that it is at the moment the war machine ceases to exist, conquered by the State, that it displays to the utmost its irreducibility, that it scatters into thinking, loving, dying, or creating machines that have at their disposal vital or revolutionary powers capable of challenging the conquering State? Is the war machine already overtaken, condemned, appropriated as part of the same process whereby it takes on new forms, undergoes a metamorphosis, affirms its irreducibility and exteriority, and deploys that milieu of pure exteriority that the occidental man of the State, or the occidental thinker, continually reduces to something other than itself? | Catatonia is « this *affect* is too strong for me », and the *fulguration*, « the force of this *affect* ***m'emporte*** <span style="color:green">[carries me off – emporter: to bear away, as booty is borne away; Massumi: "sweeps me away"]</span> », the Self being no more than a character whose gestures and emotions are desubjectified, *quitte à* dying of it. Such is the personal formula of Kleist: a succession of ***courses folles*** <span style="color:green">[mad dashes, wild races – course: a run, a race; Massumi: "flights of madness", which loses the running]</span> and frozen catatonias, where no subjective interiority any longer subsists. There is much of the Orient in Kleist: the Japanese ***lutteur*** <span style="color:green">[wrestler – the sumo wrestler, motionless and then sudden; Massumi: "fighter"]</span>, interminably immobile, then making a gesture too rapid to be perceived. The Go player. Many things in modern art come from Kleist. Goethe and Hegel are old men in relation to Kleist. Can it be that at the moment when the *machine de guerre* no longer exists, vanquished by the State, it bears witness to the highest point of its irreducibility, it ***essaime*** <span style="color:green">[swarms – essaimer: the bees' swarming off to found a new hive; Massumi: "scatters", which loses the founding]</span> into machines for thinking, loving, dying, creating, which dispose of ***forces vives*** <span style="color:green">[living forces – vis viva in physics; in politics, the active forces of a nation; Massumi: "vital powers"]</span> or revolutionary forces capable of calling the conquering State into question? It is in the same movement that the *machine de guerre* is already surpassed, condemned, appropriated, and that it takes new forms, metamorphoses itself, affirming its irreducibility, its exteriority: to deploy this milieu of pure exteriority, which the Occidental *homme d'Etat*, or the Occidental thinker, do not cease to reduce?<br>Notes: ⁹ [Minn: 11, Fr: 9] Themes analyzed by Mathieu Carrière in an unpublished study of Kleist. |
| 12¶13 fr.441 umn.356-57 | Problème I : Y a-t-il moyen de conjurer la formation d'un appareil d'Etat (ou de ses équivalents dans un groupe) ? Proposition II : L'extériorité de la machine de guerre est également attestée par l'ethnologie (hommage à la mémoire de Pierre Clastres). On a souvent défini les sociétés primitives segmentaires comme des sociétés sans Etat, c'est-à-dire où n'apparaissent pas des organes de pouvoir distincts. Mais on en concluait que ces sociétés n'ont pas atteint le degré de développement économique, ou le niveau de différenciation politique, qui rendraient à la fois possible et inévitable la formation d'un appareil d'Etat : les primitifs dès lors « ne comprennent pas » un appareil si complexe. Le premier intérêt des thèses de Clastres est de rompre avec ce postulat évolutionniste. Non seulement il doute que l'Etat soit le produit d'un développement économique assignable, mais il demande si les sociétés primitives n'ont pas le souci potentiel de conjurer et prévenir ce monstre qu'elles sont censées ne pas comprendre. Conjurer la formation d'un appareil d'Etat, rendre impossible une telle formation, serait l'objet d'un certain nombre de mécanismes sociaux primitifs, même s'ils dépassent la claire conscience. Sans doute les sociétés primitives ont-elles des chefs. Mais l'Etat ne se définit pas par l'existence de chefs, il se définit par la perpétuation ou la conservation d'organes de pouvoir. Le souci de l'Etat, c'est de conserver. Il faut donc des institutions spéciales pour qu'un chef puisse devenir homme d'Etat, mais il faut non moins des mécanismes collectifs diffus pour empêcher un chef de le devenir. Les mécanismes conjuratoires ou préventifs font partie de la chefferie, et l'empêchent de cristalliser dans un appareil distinct du corps social lui-même. | PROBLEM I. Is there a way of warding off the formation of a State apparatus (or its equivalents in a group)? PROPOSITION II. The exteriority of the war machine is also attested to by ethnology (a tribute to the memory of Pierre Clastres). Primitive, segmentary societies have often been defined as societies without a State, in other words, societies in which distinct organs of power do not appear. But the conclusion has been that these societies did not reach the degree of economic development, or the level of political differentiation, that would make the formation of the State apparatus both possible and inevitable: the implication is that primitive people "don't understand" so complex an apparatus. The prime interest in Pierre Clastres's theories is that they break with this evolutionist postulate. Not only does he doubt that the State is the product of an ascribable economic development, but he asks if it is not a potential concern of primitive societies to ward off or avert that monster they supposedly do not understand. Warding off the formation of a State apparatus, making such a formation impossible, would be the objective of a certain number of primitive social mechanisms, even if they are not consciously understood as such. To be sure, primitive societies have chiefs. But the State is not defined by the existence of chiefs; it is defined by the perpetuation or conservation of organs of power. The concern of the State is to conserve. Special institutions are thus necessary to enable a chief to become a man of State, but diffuse, collective mechanisms are just as necessary to prevent a chief from becoming one. Mechanisms for warding off, preventive mechanisms, are a part of chieftainship and keep an apparatus distinct from the social body from crystallizing. | Problem I: Is there a way to ***conjurer*** <span style="color:green">[ward off – also to conjure, to call up by incantation, and (se conjurer) to conspire: to prevent and to summon at once, the double sense Proposition XI makes a concept, « conjurer, c'est aussi anticiper »; Massumi: "warding off"]</span> the formation of an *appareil d'Etat* (or of its equivalents in a group)? Proposition II: The exteriority of the *machine de guerre* is equally *attestée* by ethnology (homage to the memory of Pierre Clastres). One has often defined primitive ***segmentaires*** <span style="color:green">[segmentary – anthropology: societies of lineage segments that fuse and divide (Evans-Pritchard), without a central organ]</span> societies as societies without State, that is to say where distinct organs of *pouvoir* do not appear. But one concluded from this that these societies have not attained the degree of economic development, or the level of political differentiation, that would render at once possible and inevitable the formation of an *appareil d'Etat*: primitives thenceforth « do not understand » so complex an *appareil*. The first interest of the theses of Clastres is to break with this evolutionist postulate. Not only does he doubt that the State is the product of an assignable economic development, but he asks whether primitive societies do not have the potential ***souci*** <span style="color:green">[care, concern – souci: worry and solicitude; the State's own word two sentences later, « le souci de l'Etat, c'est de conserver »]</span> to *conjurer* and ***prévenir*** <span style="color:green">[prevent, forestall – pré-venir: to come before, to act ahead; Massumi: "avert"]</span> this monster that they are supposed not to understand. To *conjurer* the formation of an *appareil d'Etat*, to render such a formation impossible, would be the object of a certain number of primitive social mechanisms, even if they exceed clear consciousness. Doubtless primitive societies have chiefs. But the State is not defined by the existence of chiefs, it is defined by the perpetuation or the conservation of organs of *pouvoir*. The *souci* of the State is to conserve. Special institutions are therefore needed so that a chief can become *homme d'Etat*, but no less needed are diffuse collective mechanisms to prevent a chief from becoming one. The ***conjuratoires*** <span style="color:green">[warding-off, conjuring – the adjective of conjurer: mechanisms that prevent by anticipating]</span> or preventive mechanisms form part of the ***chefferie*** <span style="color:green">[chieftainship, chiefdom – anthropology: the institution of the chief, and the type of society neo-evolutionism ranks below the State]</span>, and prevent it from ***cristalliser*** <span style="color:green">[crystallizing – chemistry: taking on a fixed, ordered form out of a solution; cf. the convergent wave of Proposition XI]</span> into an *appareil* distinct from the social body itself. |
| 12¶14 fr.441 umn.357-58 | Clastres décrit cette situation du chef qui n'a d'autre arme instituée que son prestige, pas d'autre moyen que la persuasion, pas d'autre règle que son pressentiment des désirs du groupe : le chef ressemble plus à un leader ou à une star qu'à un homme de pouvoir, et risque toujours d'être désavoué, abandonné des siens. Mais, plus encore, Clastres assigne la guerre dans les sociétés primitives comme le plus sûr mécanisme dirigé contre la formation d'Etat : c'est que la guerre maintient l'éparpillement et la segmentarité des groupes, et que le guerrier est lui-même pris dans un processus d'accumulation de ses exploits, qui le mène à une solitude et à une mort prestigieuses, mais sans pouvoir 10. Clastres peut donc se réclamer du Droit naturel, tout en en renversant la proposition principale : de même que Hobbes a bien vu que l'Etat était contre la guerre, la guerre est contre l'Etat, et le rend impossible. On n'en conclut pas que la guerre soit un état de nature, mais au contraire qu'elle est le mode d'un état social qui conjure et empêche l'Etat. La guerre primitive ne produit pas l'Etat, pas plus qu'elle n'en dérive. Et pas plus qu'elle ne s'explique par l'Etat, elle ne s'explique par l'échange : loin de dériver de l'échange, même pour en sanctionner l'échec, la guerre est ce qui limite les échanges, les maintient dans le cadre des « alliances », ce qui les empêche de devenir un facteur d'Etat, de faire fusionner les groupes. L'intérêt de cette thèse est d'abord d'attirer l'attention sur des mécanismes collectifs d'inhibition. Ces mécanismes peuvent être subtils, et fonctionner comme de micro-mécanismes. On le voit bien dans certains phénomènes de bandes ou de meutes. | Clastres describes the situation of the chief, who has no instituted weapon other than his prestige, no other means of persuasion, no other rule than his sense of the group's desires. The chief is more like a leader or a star than a man of power and is always in danger of being disavowed, abandoned by his people. But Clastres goes further, identifying war in primitive societies as the surest mechanism directed against the formation of the State: war maintains the dispersal and segmentarity of groups, and the warrior himself is caught in a process of accumulating exploits leading him to solitude and a prestigious but powerless death.¹² Clastres can thus invoke natural Law while reversing its principal proposition: just as Hobbes saw clearly that the State was against war, so war is against the State, and makes it impossible. It should not be concluded that war is a state of nature, but rather that it is the mode of a social state that wards off and prevents the State. Primitive war does not produce the State any more than it derives from it. And it is no better explained by exchange than by the State: far from deriving from exchange, even as a sanction for its failure, war is what limits exchanges, maintains them in the framework of "alliances"; it is what prevents them from becoming a State factor, from fusing groups. The importance of this thesis is first of all to draw attention to collective mechanisms of inhibition. These mechanisms may be subtle, and function as micromechanisms. This is easily seen in certain band or pack phenomena. | Clastres describes this situation of the chief who has no other instituted arm than his prestige, no other means ***que la persuasion*** <span style="color:green">[than persuasion – persuasion is his only means; Massumi: "no other means of persuasion", which makes prestige his only means of persuading]</span>, no other rule than his *pressentiment* of the desires of the group: the chief resembles a leader or a star more than a man of *pouvoir*, and always risks being disavowed, abandoned by his own. But, still more, Clastres assigns war in primitive societies as the surest mechanism directed against the formation of State: it is that war maintains the ***éparpillement*** <span style="color:green">[scattering – éparpiller: to strew, to disperse in small pieces]</span> and segmentarity of groups, and that the warrior himself is *pris* in a process of accumulation of his exploits, which leads him to a solitude and a death that are prestigious, but without *pouvoir*.¹⁰ Clastres can thus lay claim to ***Droit naturel*** <span style="color:green">[Natural Right – droit naturel: the tradition of natural right from Hobbes, not loi; Massumi: "natural Law"]</span>, while reversing its principal proposition: just as Hobbes saw clearly that the State was against war, war is against the State, and renders it impossible. One does not conclude from this that war is a state of nature, but on the contrary that it is the mode of a social state that *conjure* and prevents the State. Primitive war does not produce the State, any more than it derives from it. And no more than it is explained by the State, is it explained by exchange: far from deriving from exchange, even to sanction its failure, war is what limits exchanges, maintains them in the frame of « alliances », what prevents them from becoming a factor of State, from making groups fuse. The interest of this thesis is first to draw attention to collective mechanisms of inhibition. These mechanisms can be subtle, and function as micro-mechanisms. One sees it clearly in certain phenomena of bands or of *meutes*. |
