| 12¶15 fr.441 umn.358 | Par exemple, à propos des bandes de gamins de Bogota, Jacques Meunier cite trois moyens qui empêchent le leader d'acquérir un pouvoir stable : les membres de la bande se réunissent, et mènent leur activité de vol en commun, avec butin collectif, mais ils se dispersent, ne restent pas ensemble pour dormir et manger ; d'autre part et surtout, chaque membre de la bande est apparié à un, deux ou trois autres membres, si bien que, en cas de désaccord avec le chef, il ne partira pas seul, mais entraîne ses alliés dont le départ conjugué risque de disloquer la bande entière ; enfin il y a une limite d'âge diffuse qui fait que, vers [...] 10. Pierre Clastres, La société contre l'Etat, Ed. de Minuit ; « Archéologie de la violence » et « Malheur du guerrier sauvage », in Libre 1 et 2, Payot. C'est dans ce dernier texte que Clastres fait le portrait du destin du guerrier dans la société primitive, et analyse le mécanisme qui empêche la concentration de pouvoir (de même, Mauss avait montré dans le potlatch un mécanisme empêchant la concentration de richesse). [...] quinze ans, on est forcément amené à quitter la bande, à en décoller 11. Pour comprendre ces mécanismes, il faut renoncer à la vision évolutionniste qui fait de la bande ou de la meute une forme sociale rudimentaire et moins bien organisée. Même dans les bandes animales, la chefferie est un mécanisme complexe qui ne promeut pas le plus fort, mais inhibe plutôt l'installation de pouvoirs stables au profit d'un tissu de relations immanentes 12. | For example, in the case of gangs of street children in Bogota, Jacques Meunier cites three ways in which the leader is prevented from acquiring stable power: the members of the band meet and undertake their theft activity in common, with collective sharing of the loot, but they disperse to eat or sleep separately; also, and especially, each member of the band is paired off with one, two, or three other members, so if he has a disagreement with the leader, he will not leave alone but will take along his allies, whose combined departure will threaten to break up the entire gang; finally, there is a diffuse age limit, and at about age fifteen a member is inevitably induced to quit the gang.¹³ These mechanisms cannot be understood without renouncing the evolutionist vision that sees bands or packs as a rudimentary, less organized, social form. Even in bands of animals, leadership is a complex mechanism that does not act to promote the strongest but rather inhibits the installation of stable powers, in favor of a fabric of immanent relations. | For example, apropos of the bands of street kids in Bogotá, Jacques Meunier cites three means that prevent the leader from acquiring a stable *pouvoir*: the members of the band come together, and carry on their activity of theft in common, with collective ***butin*** <span style="color:green">[booty, spoils – the war-capture proper, set against State capture in plateau 13]</span>, but they disperse, do not stay together to sleep and eat; on the other hand and above all, each member of the band is paired with one, two, or three other members, so that, in case of disagreement with the chief, he will not leave alone, but draws along his allies, whose conjugated departure risks ***disloquer*** <span style="color:green">[dislocating – disjointing, as a joint is dislocated; Massumi: "break up"]</span> the entire band; finally there is a diffuse age limit such that, around fifteen, one is necessarily led to leave the band, to ***décoller*** <span style="color:green">[come unstuck from it – décoller: to unstick, and of a plane, to take off; Massumi: "quit the gang"]</span> from it.¹¹ To understand these mechanisms, one must renounce the evolutionist vision that makes of the band or the *meute* a rudimentary and less well organized social form. Even in animal bands, the *chefferie* is a complex mechanism that does not promote the strongest, but rather inhibits the installation of stable *pouvoirs* to the profit of a ***tissu*** <span style="color:green">[fabric, tissue – a weave; also the tissue of a living body]</span> of immanent relations.¹²<br>Notes: ¹⁰ [Minn: 12, Fr: 10] Pierre Clastres, <u>La société contre l'Etat</u>, Éd. de Minuit; « Archéologie de la violence » and « Malheur du guerrier sauvage », in <u>Libre</u> 1 and 2, Payot. It is in this last text that Clastres paints the portrait of the destiny of the warrior in primitive society, and analyzes the mechanism that prevents the concentration of *pouvoir* (in the same way, Mauss had shown in the potlatch a mechanism preventing the concentration of wealth). [English: <u>Society against the State</u>, trans. Robert Hurley, Urizen 1977, Zone 1987; both essays in <u>Archeology of Violence</u>, trans. Jeanine Herman, Semiotext(e), 1994.] |
| 12¶16 fr.441 umn.358-59 | On pourrait aussi bien opposer chez les hommes les plus évolués la forme de « mondanité » à celle de « sociabilité » : les groupes mondains sont proches des bandes et procèdent par diffusion de prestige, plutôt que par référence à des centres de pouvoir comme dans des groupes sociaux (Proust a bien montré cette non-correspondance des valeurs mondaines et des valeurs sociales). Eugène Sue, mondain et dandy, à qui les légitimistes reprochaient de fréquenter la famille d'Orléans, disait : « Je ne me rallie pas à la famille, je me rallie à la meute. » Les meutes, les bandes sont des groupes du type rhizome, par opposition au type arborescent qui se concentre sur des organes de pouvoir. C'est pourquoi les bandes en général, même de brigandage, ou de mondanité, sont des métamorphoses d'une machine de guerre, laquelle diffère formellement de tout appareil d'Etat, ou équivalent, qui structure au contraire les sociétés centralisées. On ne dira certes pas que la discipline est le propre de la machine de guerre : la discipline devient le caractère exigé des armées, quand l'Etat se les approprie ; mais la machine de guerre répond à d'autres règles dont nous ne disons certes pas qu'elles valent mieux, mais qu'elles animent une indiscipline fondamentale du guerrier, une remise en question de la hiérarchie, un chantage perpétuel à l'abandon et à la trahison, un sens de l'honneur très susceptible, et qui contrarie, encore une fois, la formation d'Etat. Qu'est-ce qui fait cependant que cette thèse ne nous convainc pas complètement ? Nous suivons Clastres lorsqu'il montre que l'Etat ne s'explique pas par un développement des forces productives, ni par une différenciation des forces politiques. | One could just as easily compare the form "high-society life" to the form "sociability" among the most highly evolved men and women: high-society groups are similar to gangs and operate by the diffusion of prestige rather than by reference to centers of power, as in social groupings (Proust clearly showed this noncorrespondence of high-society values and social values). Eugene Sue, a man of high society and a dandy, whom legitimists reproached for frequenting the Orleans family, used to say: "I'm not on the side of the family, I side with the pack." Packs, bands, are groups of the rhizome type, as opposed to the arborescent type that centers around organs of power. That is why bands in general, even those engaged in banditry or high-society life, are metamorphoses of a war machine formally distinct from all State apparatuses or their equivalents, which are instead what structure centralized societies. We certainly would not say that discipline is what defines a war machine: discipline is the characteristic required of armies after the State has appropriated them. The war machine answers to other rules. We are not saying that they are better, of course, only that they animate a fundamental indiscipline of the warrior, a questioning of hierarchy, perpetual blackmail by abandonment or betrayal, and a very volatile sense of honor, all of which, once again, impedes the formation of the State. But why does this argument fail to convince us entirely? We follow Clastres when he demonstrates that the State is explained neither by a development of productive forces nor by a differentiation of political forces. | One could just as well oppose, among the most evolved humans, the form of « ***mondanité*** <span style="color:green">[society life, worldliness – le monde, high society; Massumi: "high-society life"]</span> » to that of « sociability »: worldly groups are close to bands and proceed by diffusion of prestige, rather than by reference to centers of *pouvoir* as in social groups (Proust showed well this non-correspondence of worldly values and social values). Eugène Sue, worldly man and dandy, whom the legitimists reproached for frequenting the Orléans family, used to say: « I do not ***me rallie*** <span style="color:green">[rally to – se rallier: to declare allegiance, as royalists rallied to a pretender; Massumi: "side with"]</span> to the family, I *me rallie* to the *meute*. » *Meutes*, bands, are groups of the ***rhizome*** <span style="color:green">[rhizome – botany: a creeping rootstock without trunk or center; the concept of plateau 1, against the arborescent tree]</span> type, as opposed to the ***arborescent*** <span style="color:green">[tree-like – branching from a trunk and root: hierarchy, center, filiation]</span> type that concentrates itself on organs of *pouvoir*. That is why bands in general, even of *brigandage*, or of *mondanité*, are metamorphoses of a *machine de guerre*, which differs formally from every *appareil d'Etat*, or equivalent, which on the contrary structures centralized societies. ***On ne dira certes pas*** <span style="color:green">[one will certainly not say – impersonal on + future of instruction, as in plateau 13's « on ne confondra certes pas »]</span> that discipline is *le propre* of the *machine de guerre*: discipline becomes the character required of armies, when the State appropriates them; but the *machine de guerre* answers to other rules, of which we certainly do not say that they are worth more, but that they animate a fundamental indiscipline of the warrior, a calling-into-question of hierarchy, a perpetual ***chantage*** <span style="color:green">[blackmail – the threat held over another; chantage à l'abandon: blackmail by threat of desertion]</span> of abandonment and betrayal, a sense of honor very ***susceptible*** <span style="color:green">[touchy, quick to take offence – Massumi: "volatile"]</span>, and which thwarts, once again, the formation of State. What is it, however, that makes this thesis not convince us completely? We follow Clastres when he shows that the State is not explained by a development of the productive forces, nor by a differentiation of the political forces. |
| 12¶17 fr.441 umn.359 | C'est lui qui rend possible au contraire l'entreprise des grands travaux, la cons[...] 11. Jacques Meunier, Les gamins de Bogota, Lattès, p. 159 (« chantage à la dispersion »), p. 177 : au besoin, « ce sont les autres gamins, par un jeu compliqué de vexations et de silences, qui le pénètrent de l'idée qu'il doit quitter la bande ». Meunier souligne à quel point le destin de l'ex-gamin est compromis : non seulement pour des raisons de santé, mais parce qu'il s'intègre mal à la « pègre », laquelle est pour lui une société trop hiérarchisée, trop centralisée, trop centrée sur des organes de pouvoir (p. 178). Sur les bandes d'enfants, cf. aussi le roman d'Amado, Capitaines des sables, Gallimard. 12. Cf. I. S. Bernstein, « La dominance sociale chez les primates », in La Recherche n° 91, juillet 1978. [...] titution des surplus, et l'organisation des fonctions publiques correspondantes. C'est lui qui rend possible la distinction des gouvernants et des gouvernés. On ne voit pas comment expliquer l'Etat par ce qui le suppose, même en recourant à la dialectique. Il semble bien que l'Etat surgisse d'un coup, sous une forme impériale, et ne renvoie pas à des facteurs progressifs. Son surgissement sur place est comme un coup de génie, la naissance d'Athéna. Nous suivons Clastres également lorsqu'il montre qu'une machine de guerre est dirigée contre l'Etat, soit contre des Etats potentiels dont elle conjure la formation d'avance, soit, plus encore, contre les Etats actuels dont elle se propose la destruction. En effet, la machine de guerre est sans doute effectuée dans les agencements « barbares » des nomades guerriers, beaucoup plus que dans les agencements « sauvages » des sociétés primitives. | It is the State, on the contrary, that makes possible the undertaking of large-scale projects, the constitution of surpluses, and the organization of the corresponding public functions. The State is what makes the distinction between governors and governed possible. We do not see how the State can be explained by what it presupposes, even with recourse to dialectics. The State seems to rise up in a single stroke, in an imperial form, and does not depend on progressive factors. Its on-the-spot emergence is like a stroke of genius, the birth of Athena. We also follow Clastres when he shows that the war machine is directed against the State, either against potential States whose formation it wards off in advance, or against actual States whose destruction it purposes. No doubt the war machine is realized more completely in the "barbaric" assemblages of nomadic warriors than in the "savage" assemblages of primitive societies. | It is the State, on the contrary, that renders possible the enterprise of *grands travaux*, the constitution of surpluses, and the organization of the corresponding public functions. It is the State that renders possible the distinction of governors and governed. One does not see how to explain the State by what presupposes it, even by recourse to the dialectic. It seems indeed that the State *surgisse* ***d'un coup*** <span style="color:green">[at a stroke, all at once – coup: blow, stroke; the State's « coup once and for all » of plateau 13]</span>, under an imperial form, and does not refer back to progressive factors. Its ***surgissement sur place*** <span style="color:green">[surging-up on the spot – sur place: right there, without moving; the phrase Deleuze uses for « nomades sur place »]</span> is like a stroke of genius, the birth of Athena. We follow Clastres equally when he shows that a *machine de guerre* is directed against the State, either against potential States whose formation it *conjure* in advance, or, still more, against actual States whose destruction it proposes to itself. In effect, the *machine de guerre* is doubtless ***effectuée*** <span style="color:green">[effectuated – brought into effect, actualized: the virtual made real in a state of affairs; Massumi: "realized"]</span> in the « ***barbares*** <span style="color:green">[barbarian – the middle term of Morgan's savagery-barbarism-civilization, reworked in Anti-Oedipus as the despotic barbarian]</span> » *agencements* of warrior nomads, much more than in the « ***sauvages*** <span style="color:green">[savage – the first term of the same sequence, the primitive territorial machine of Anti-Oedipus]</span> » *agencements* of primitive societies.<br>Notes: ¹¹ [Minn: 13, Fr: 11] Jacques Meunier, <u>Les gamins de Bogota</u>, Lattès, p. 159 (« blackmail by dispersion »), p. 177: if need be, « it is the other kids, by a complicated play of vexations and silences, who penetrate him with the idea that he must leave the band ». Meunier underlines to what point the destiny of the ex-kid is compromised: not only for reasons of health, but because he integrates badly into the « underworld », which is for him a society too hierarchized, too centralized, too centered on organs of *pouvoir* (p. 178). On bands of children, cf. also Amado's novel, <u>Capitaines des sables</u>, Gallimard. [No English translation of Meunier; Amado: <u>Captains of the Sands</u>.] ¹² [Minn: 14, Fr: 12] Cf. I. S. Bernstein, « La dominance sociale chez les primates », in <u>La Recherche</u> no. 91, July 1978. |
| 12¶18 fr.441 umn.359 | En tout cas, il est exclu que la guerre produise un Etat, ou que l'Etat soit le résultat d'une guerre dont les vainqueurs imposeraient par là même une loi nouvelle aux vaincus, puisque l'organisation de la machine de guerre est dirigée contre la forme-Etat, actuelle ou virtuelle. On ne rend pas mieux compte de l'Etat par un résultat de la guerre que par une progression de forces économiques ou politiques. Dès lors, Pierre Clastres creuse la coupure : entre des sociétés contre-Etat, dites primitives, et des sociétés-à-Etat, dites monstrueuses, dont on ne voit plus du tout comment elles ont pu se former. Clastres est fasciné par le problème d'une « servitude volontaire », à la manière de La Boétie : comment des gens ont-ils voulu ou désiré une servitude, qui ne leur venait certes pas d'une issue de guerre involontaire et malheureuse ? Ils disposaient pourtant de mécanismes contre-Etat : alors, pourquoi et comment l'Etat ? Pourquoi l'Etat a-t-il triomphé ? Pierre Clastres, à force d'approfondir ce problème, semblait se priver des moyens de le résoudre 13. Il tendait à faire des sociétés primitives une hypostase, une entité auto-suffisante (il insistait beaucoup sur ce point). De l'extériorité formelle, il [...] 13. Clastres, La société contre l'Etat, p. 170 : « L'apparition de l'Etat a opéré le grand partage typologique entre Sauvages et Civilisés, elle a inscrit l'ineffaçable coupure dans l'au-delà de laquelle tout est changé, car le temps devient Histoire. » | In any case, it is out of the question that the State could be the result of a war in which the conquerors imposed, by the very fact of their victory, a new law on the vanquished, because the organization of the war machine is directed against the State-form, actual or virtual. The State is no better accounted for as a result of war than by a progression of economic or political forces. This is where Clastres locates the break: between "primitive" counter-State societies and "monstrous" State societies whose formation it is no longer possible to explain. Clastres is fascinated by the problem of "voluntary servitude," in the manner of La Boetie: In what way did people want or desire servitude, which most certainly did not come to them as the outcome of an involuntary and unfortunate war? They did, after all, have counter-State mechanisms at their disposal: So how and why the State? Why did the State triumph? The more deeply Clastres delved into the problem, the more he seemed to deprive himself of the means of resolving it.¹⁵ He tended to make primitive societies hypostases, self-sufficient entities (he insisted heavily on this point). | In any case, it is excluded that war produces a State, or that the State is the result of a war whose victors would by that very fact impose a new law on the vanquished, since the organization of the *machine de guerre* is directed against the ***forme-Etat*** <span style="color:green">[State-form – the form of interiority as such, actual or virtual, not this or that State]</span>, actual or virtual. One does not give a better account of the State by a result of war than by a progression of economic or political forces. Thenceforth, Pierre Clastres ***creuse la coupure*** <span style="color:green">[deepens the cut, digs the break – creuser: to hollow out, to widen a ditch; Massumi: "locates the break", which loses that Clastres makes it deeper]</span>: between counter-State societies, ***dites*** <span style="color:green">[so-called – dites: said to be, labeled; the label belongs to others; Massumi turns it into scare quotes]</span> primitive, and societies-with-State, *dites* monstrous, of which one no longer sees at all how they were able to form. Clastres is fascinated by the problem of a « voluntary servitude », in the manner of La Boétie: how did people want or desire a servitude, which certainly did not come to them from an involuntary and unfortunate ***issue*** <span style="color:green">[outcome – the issue of a war, how it came out; also an exit]</span> of war? They had at their disposal, however, counter-State mechanisms: then, why and how the State? Why did the State triumph? Pierre Clastres, ***à force d'approfondir*** <span style="color:green">[by dint of deepening – à force de: by sheer persistence in; the same digging as creuser]</span> this problem, seemed to deprive himself of the means to resolve it.¹³ He tended to make of primitive societies a ***hypostase*** <span style="color:green">[hypostasis – theology and philosophy: an abstraction made into a substance standing on its own]</span>, a self-sufficient entity (he insisted a great deal on this point). Of formal exteriority, he<br>Notes: ¹³ [Minn: 15, Fr: 13] Clastres, <u>La société contre l'Etat</u>, p. 170: « The appearance of the State operated the great typological division between Savages and Civilized, it inscribed the ineffaceable cut beyond which everything is changed, for time becomes History. » Hurley's English (<u>Society against the State</u>, Zone, 1987, in the chapter on the typology of societies, after p. 198 of the scan; OCR corrected): “The emergence of the State brought about the great typological division between Savage and Civilized man; it created the unbridgeable gulf whereby everything was changed, for, on the other side, Time became History.” |
| 12¶19 fr.441 umn.359-60 | Pour rendre compte de cette apparition, Clastres invoquait d'abord un facteur démographique (mais « sans songer à substituer à un déterminisme économique un déterminisme démographique… ») ; et aussi l'emballement éventuel de la machine guerrière (?) ; ou bien, d'une manière plus inattendue, le rôle indirect d'un certain prophétisme qui, d'abord dirigé contre les « chefs », aurait produit un pouvoir autrement redoutable. Mais on ne peut évidemment pas préjuger des solutions plus élaborées que Clastres aurait données à ce problème. Sur le rôle éventuel du prophétisme, on se reportera au livre d'Hélène Clastres, La terre sans mal, le prophétisme tupi-guarani, Ed. du Seuil. [...] faisait une indépendance réelle. Par là il restait évolutionniste, et se donnait un état de nature. Seulement, cet état de nature était selon lui une réalité pleinement sociale, au lieu d'un pur concept, et cette évolution était de mutation brusque, au lieu de développement. Car, d'une part, l'Etat surgissait tout d'un coup, tout fait ; d'autre part, les sociétés contre-Etat disposaient de mécanismes très précis pour le conjurer, pour empêcher qu'il ne surgisse. Nous croyons que ces deux propositions sont bonnes, mais que leur enchaînement fait défaut. Il y a un vieux schéma : « des clans aux empires », ou « des bandes aux royaumes »… Mais rien ne nous dit qu'il y ait une évolution en ce sens, puisque les bandes et les clans ne sont pas moins organisés que les royaumes-empires. Or on ne rompra pas avec cette hypothèse d'évolution en creusant la coupure entre les deux termes, c'est-à-dire en donnant une auto-suffisance aux bandes, et un surgissement d'autant plus miraculeux ou monstrueux à l'Etat. Il faut dire que l'Etat, il y en a toujours eu, et très parfait, très formé. | He made their formal exteriority into a real independence. Thus he remained an evolutionist, and posited a state of nature. Only this state of nature was, according to him, a fully social reality instead of a pure concept, and the evolution was a sudden mutation instead of a development. For on the one hand, the State rises up in a single stroke, fully formed; on the other, the counter-State societies use very specific mechanisms to ward it off, to prevent it from arising. We believe that these two propositions are valid but that their interlinkage is flawed. There is an old scenario: "from clans to empires," or "from bands to kingdoms." But nothing says that this constitutes an evolution, since bands and clans are no less organized than empire-kingdoms. We will never leave the evolution hypothesis behind by creating a break between the two terms, that is, by endowing bands with self-sufficiency and the State with an emergence all the more miraculous and monstrous. We are compelled to say that there has always been a State, quite perfect, quite complete. | made a real independence. By this he remained an evolutionist, and ***se donnait*** <span style="color:green">[gave himself, granted himself – se donner: to presuppose as given; Massumi: "posited"]</span> a state of nature. Only, this state of nature was according to him a fully social reality, instead of a pure concept, and this evolution was one of ***mutation brusque*** <span style="color:green">[abrupt mutation – biology: the sudden mutation (de Vries) against Darwinian gradual variation]</span>, instead of development. For, on the one hand, the State *surgissait* all at once, ***tout fait*** <span style="color:green">[ready-made, already made – tout fait: as one says a ready-made garment; Massumi: "fully formed"]</span>; on the other hand, counter-State societies disposed of very precise mechanisms to *conjurer* it, to prevent it from *surgir*. We believe that these two propositions are good, but that their ***enchaînement fait défaut*** <span style="color:green">[their linking is lacking – faire défaut: to be missing, to default; Massumi: "interlinkage is flawed", which supposes a link that is there]</span>. There is an old schema: « from clans to empires », or « from bands to kingdoms »… But nothing tells us that there is an evolution in this sense, since bands and clans are not less organized than kingdom-empires. Now, one will not break with this hypothesis of evolution by *creusant la coupure* between the two terms, that is to say by giving a self-sufficiency to bands, and a *surgissement* all the more miraculous or monstrous to the State. ***Il faut dire que*** <span style="color:green">[it must be said that – the plain constative; Massumi: "We are compelled to say", which adds a compulsion]</span> the State, there has always been one, and very perfect, very ***formé*** <span style="color:green">[formed – fully shaped; set against the tout fait just above]</span>.<br>Notes: ¹³ (cont.) To account for this appearance, Clastres invoked first a demographic factor (but « without dreaming of substituting for an economic determinism a demographic determinism… »); and also the eventual ***emballement*** <span style="color:green">[runaway, racing – of an engine that races out of control; of a horse that bolts]</span> of the warrior machine (?); or else, in a more unexpected manner, the indirect role of a certain ***prophétisme*** <span style="color:green">[prophetism – the Tupi-Guarani prophets (karai) who led migrations toward the Land without Evil]</span> which, first directed against the « chiefs », would have produced a *pouvoir* otherwise formidable. But one evidently cannot prejudge the more elaborated solutions that Clastres would have given to this problem. On the eventual role of prophetism, one will refer to the book of Hélène Clastres, <u>La terre sans mal, le prophétisme tupi-guarani</u>, Éd. du Seuil. [English: <u>The Land-Without-Evil: Tupí-Guaraní Prophetism</u>, trans. Jacqueline Grenez Brovender, Illinois, 1995.] |
| 12¶20 fr.441 umn.360 | Plus les archéologues font de découvertes, plus ils découvrent des empires. L'hypothèse de l'Urstaat semble vérifiée, « l'Etat bien compris remonte déjà aux temps les plus reculés de l'humanité ». Nous n'imaginons guère de sociétés primitives qui n'aient été en contact avec des Etats impériaux, à la périphérie ou dans des zones mal contrôlées. Mais le plus important, c'est l'hypothèse inverse : que l'Etat lui-même a toujours été en rapport avec un dehors, et n'est pas pensable indépendamment de ce rapport. La loi de l'Etat n'est pas celle du Tout ou Rien (sociétés à Etat ou sociétés contre Etat), mais celle de l'intérieur et de l'extérieur. L'Etat, c'est la souveraineté. Mais la souveraineté ne règne que sur ce qu'elle est capable d'intérioriser, de s'approprier localement. Non seulement il n'y a pas d'Etat universel, mais le dehors des Etats ne se laisse pas réduire à la « politique extérieure », c'est-à-dire à un ensemble de rapports entre les Etats. Le dehors apparaît simultanément dans deux directions : de grandes machines mondiales, ramifiées sur tout l'œcumène à un moment donné, et qui jouissent d'une large autonomie par rapport aux Etats (par exemple, des organisations commerciales du type « grandes compagnies », ou bien des complexes industriels, ou même des formations religieuses comme le christianisme, l'islamisme, certains mouvements de prophétisme ou de messianisme, etc.) ; mais, aussi, des mécanismes locaux de bandes, marges, minorités, qui continuent d'affirmer les droits de sociétés segmentaires contre les organes de pouvoir d'Etat. | The more discoveries archaeologists make, the more empires they uncover. The hypothesis of the Urstaat seems to be verified: "The State clearly dates back to the most remote ages of humanity." It is hard to imagine primitive societies that would not have been in contact with imperial States, at the periphery or in poorly controlled areas. But of greater importance is the inverse hypothesis: that the State itself has always been in a relation with an outside and is inconceivable independent of that relationship. The law of the State is not the law of All or Nothing (State societies or counter-State societies) but that of interior and exterior. The State is sovereignty. But sovereignty only reigns over what it is capable of internalizing, of appropriating locally. Not only is there no universal State, but the outside of States cannot be reduced to "foreign policy," that is, to a set of relations among States. The outside appears simultaneously in two directions: huge worldwide machines branched out over the entire ecumenon at a given moment, which enjoy a large measure of autonomy in relation to the States (for example, commercial organization of the "multinational" type, or industrial complexes, or even religious formations like Christianity, Islam, certain prophetic or messianic movements, etc.); but also the local mechanisms of bands, margins, minorities, which continue to affirm the rights of segmentary societies in opposition to the organs of State power. | The more discoveries archaeologists make, the more empires they discover. The hypothesis of the ***Urstaat*** <span style="color:green">[German: primordial State – Anti-Oedipus's despotic State that appears all at once, already complete; Massumi keeps the German]</span> seems verified, « the State properly understood goes back already to the most remote times of humanity ». We hardly imagine primitive societies that would not have been in contact with imperial States, at the periphery or in poorly controlled zones. But the most important is the inverse hypothesis: that the State itself has always been in relation with a *dehors*, and is not ***pensable*** <span style="color:green">[thinkable – penser again; Massumi: "inconceivable"]</span> independently of this relation. The law of the State is not that of ***Tout ou Rien*** <span style="color:green">[All or Nothing – the binary of having a State or not, as in the all-or-nothing law of a nerve that fires or does not]</span> (societies with State or societies against State), but that of the interior and the exterior. The State is sovereignty. But sovereignty reigns only over what it is capable of ***intérioriser*** <span style="color:green">[interiorizing – taking into the form of interiority; Massumi: "internalizing"]</span>, of appropriating to itself locally. Not only is there no universal State, but the *dehors* of States does not let itself be reduced to « foreign policy », that is to say to a set of relations between States. The *dehors* appears simultaneously in two directions: great world machines, ***ramifiées*** <span style="color:green">[ramified, branched – ramus, branch; Massumi: "branched out"]</span> over the whole ***œcumène*** <span style="color:green">[ecumene – Greek oikoumenē: the inhabited world, the known earth of the geographers]</span> at a given moment, and which enjoy a large autonomy in relation to States (for example, commercial organizations of the « ***grandes compagnies*** <span style="color:green">[great companies – the chartered trading companies of the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries, the East India Companies; Massumi: "multinational", which moves the example forward three centuries]</span> » type, or else industrial complexes, or even religious formations like Christianity, Islam, certain movements of *prophétisme* or of messianism, etc.); but, also, local mechanisms of bands, ***marges*** <span style="color:green">[margins – the edges of a page or a society; the marginal]</span>, minorities, which continue to affirm the rights of segmentary societies against the organs of State *pouvoir*. |
| 12¶21 fr.441 umn.360 | Le monde moderne peut nous présenter aujourd'hui des images particulièrement développées de ces deux directions, du côté des machines mondiales œcuméniques, mais aussi vers un néo-primitivisme, une nouvelle société tribale telle que la décrit Mac Luhan. Ces directions n'en sont pas moins présentes dans tout champ social, et de tout temps. Il arrive même qu'elles se confondent partiellement ; par exemple, une organisation commerciale est aussi une bande de pillage ou de piraterie, sur une partie de son parcours et dans beaucoup de ses activités ; ou bien c'est par bandes qu'une formation religieuse commence à opérer. Ce qui apparaît, c'est que les bandes non moins que les organisations mondiales impliquent une forme irréductible à l'Etat, et que cette forme d'extériorité se présente nécessairement comme celle d'une machine de guerre, polymorphe et diffuse. C'est un nomos, très différent de la « loi ». La forme-Etat, comme forme d'intériorité, a une tendance à se reproduire, identique à soi à travers ses variations, aisément reconnaissable dans les limites de ses pôles, s'adressant toujours à la recognition publique (il n'y a pas d'Etat masqué). Mais la forme d'extériorité de la machine de guerre fait qu'elle n'existe que dans ses propres métamorphoses ; elle existe aussi bien dans une innovation industrielle, dans une invention technologique, dans un circuit commercial, dans une création religieuse, dans tous ces flux et courants qui ne se laissent approprier par les Etats que secondairement. | The modern world can provide us today with particularly well developed images of these two directions: worldwide ecumenical machines, but also a neoprimitivism, a new tribal society as described by Marshall McLuhan. These directions are equally present in all social fields, in all periods. It even happens that they partially merge. For example, a commercial organization is also a band of pillage, or piracy, for part of its course and in many of its activities; or it is in bands that a religious formation begins to operate. What becomes clear is that bands, no less than worldwide organizations, imply a form irreducible to the State and that this form of exteriority necessarily presents itself as a diffuse and polymorphous war machine. It is a nomos very different from the "law." The State-form, as a form of interiority, has a tendency to reproduce itself, remaining identical to itself across its variations and easily recognizable within the limits of its poles, always seeking public recognition (there is no masked State). But the war machine's form of exteriority is such that it exists only in its own metamorphoses; it exists in an industrial innovation as well as in a technological invention, in a commercial circuit as well as in a religious creation, in all flows and currents that only secondarily allow themselves to be appropriated by the State. | The modern world can present us today with particularly developed images of these two directions, on the side of ecumenical world machines, but also toward a neo-***primitivisme*** <span style="color:green">[primitivism – McLuhan's retribalized global village of electric media]</span>, a new tribal society such as McLuhan describes it. These directions are no less present in every social field, and at all times. It even happens that they partially *se confondent*; for example, a commercial organization is also a band of ***pillage*** <span style="color:green">[pillage, plunder – the sack of a town]</span> or of piracy, over a part of its ***parcours*** <span style="color:green">[course, route – the path traveled; the trajet of Proposition V]</span> and in many of its activities; or else it is by bands that a religious formation begins to operate. What appears is that bands no less than world organizations imply a form irreducible to the State, and that this form of exteriority presents itself necessarily as that of a *machine de guerre*, polymorphous and diffuse. It is a *nomos*, very different from the « ***loi*** <span style="color:green">[law – the State's law, lex, against nomos as distribution]</span> ». The *forme-Etat*, as form of interiority, has a tendency to reproduce itself, identical to itself across its variations, easily recognizable within the limits of its poles, always addressing itself to public ***recognition*** <span style="color:green">[recognition – the philosophers' word (Kant's synthesis of recognition, the image of thought in Difference and Repetition), not the everyday reconnaissance]</span> (there is no ***Etat masqué*** <span style="color:green">[masked State – the State always shows its face, against the secret of the war machine]</span>). But the form of exteriority of the *machine de guerre* makes it exist only in its own metamorphoses; it exists just as well in an industrial innovation, in a technological invention, in a commercial circuit, in a religious creation, in all these *flux* and currents that let themselves be appropriated by States only secondarily. |
| 12¶22 fr.441 umn.360-61 | Ce n'est pas en termes d'indépendance, mais de coexistence et de concurrence, dans un champ perpétuel d'interaction, qu'il faut penser l'extériorité et l'intériorité, les machines de guerre à métamorphoses et les appareils identitaires d'Etat, les bandes et les royaumes, les mégamachines et les empires. Un même champ circonscrit son intériorité dans des Etats, mais décrit son extériorité dans ce qui échappe aux Etats ou se dresse contre les Etats. | It is in terms not of independence, but of coexistence and competition in a perpetual field of interaction, that we must conceive of exteriority and interiority, war machines of metamorphosis and State apparatuses of identity, bands and kingdoms, megamachines and empires. The same field circumscribes its interiority in States, but describes its exteriority in what escapes States or stands against States. | It is not in terms of independence, but of coexistence and of ***concurrence*** <span style="color:green">[competition, rivalry – also concurrence in the old sense of running together, converging]</span>, in a perpetual field of interaction, that one must *penser* exteriority and interiority, the *machines de guerre* ***à métamorphoses*** <span style="color:green">[with metamorphoses, metamorphic – à as in a machine à vapeur: running on metamorphoses]</span> and the ***identitaires*** <span style="color:green">[identitarian – producing and guarding identity; Massumi: "of identity"]</span> *appareils d'Etat*, the bands and the kingdoms, the ***mégamachines*** <span style="color:green">[megamachines – Lewis Mumford's term for the archaic State as a machine made of human parts (The Myth of the Machine, 1967)]</span> and the empires. One same field ***circonscrit*** <span style="color:green">[circumscribes – geometry: draws a figure around another; set against décrit, which traces a curve]</span> its interiority in States, but ***décrit*** <span style="color:green">[describes – geometry: traces, as a moving point describes a curve]</span> its exteriority in what escapes States or stands up against States. |
| 12¶23 fr.446 umn.361 | Proposition III : L'extériorité de la machine de guerre est encore attestée par l'épistémologie, qui laisse pressentir l'existence et la perpétuation d'une « science mineure » ou « nomade ». Il y a un genre de science, ou un traitement de la science, qui semble très difficile à classer, et dont il est même difficile de suivre l'histoire. Ce ne sont pas des « techniques », suivant l'acception coutumière. Mais ce ne sont pas non plus des « sciences », au sens royal ou légal établi par l'histoire. D'après un livre récent de Michel Serres, on peut en repérer la trace à la fois dans la physique atomique, de Démocrite à Lucrèce, et dans la géométrie d'Archimède 14. Les caractères d'une telle science excentrique [...] 14. Michel Serres, La naissance de la physique dans le texte de Lucrèce. Fleuves et turbulences, Ed. de Minuit. Serres est le premier à dégager les trois points qui suivent ; le quatrième nous semble s'enchaîner avec eux. [...] seraient les suivants : 1) Elle aurait d'abord un modèle hydraulique, au lieu d'être une théorie des solides considérant les fluides comme un cas particulier ; en effet, l'atomisme antique n'est pas séparable des flux, le flux est la réalité même ou la consistance. 2) C'est un modèle de devenir et d'hétérogénéité, qui s'oppose au stable, à l'éternel, à l'identique, au constant. C'est un « paradoxe », faire du devenir lui-même un modèle, et non plus le caractère second d'une copie ; Platon, dans le Timée, évoquait cette possibilité, mais pour l'exclure et la conjurer, au nom de la science royale. Or, dans l'atomisme, au contraire, la fameuse déclinaison de l'atome fournit un tel modèle d'hétérogénéité, et de passage ou de devenir dans l'hétérogène. | PROPOSITION III. The exteriority of the war machine is also attested to by epistemology, which intimates the existence and perpetuation of a "nomad" or "minor science." There is a kind of science, or treatment of science, that seems very difficult to classify, whose history is even difficult to follow. What we are referring to are not "technologies" in the usual sense of the term. But neither are they "sciences" in the royal or legal sense established by history. According to a recent book by Michel Serres, both the atomic physics of Democritus and Lucretius and the geometry of Archimedes are marked by it.¹⁶ The characteristics of this kind of eccentric science would seem to be the following: 1. First of all, it uses a hydraulic model, rather than being a theory of solids treating fluids as a special case; ancient atomism is inseparable from flows, and flux is reality itself, or consistency. 2. The model in question is one of becoming and heterogeneity, as opposed to the stable, the eternal, the identical, the constant. It is a "paradox" to make becoming itself a model, and no longer a secondary characteristic, a copy; in the Timaeus, Plato raises this possibility, but only in order to exclude it and conjure it away in the name of royal science. By contrast, in atomism, just such a model of heterogeneity, and of passage or becoming in the heterogeneous, is furnished by the famed declination of the atom. | Proposition III: The exteriority of the *machine de guerre* is again *attestée* by epistemology, which ***laisse pressentir*** <span style="color:green">[lets one have a presentiment of – pressentir again (12¶9, 12¶14); Massumi: "intimates"]</span> the existence and perpetuation of a « ***science mineure*** <span style="color:green">[minor science – mineure as in Kafka's minor literature (1975): not lesser, but a variation working inside a major language]</span> » or « ***nomade*** <span style="color:green">[nomad – D&G put minor first; Massumi reverses the order: "nomad" or "minor science"]</span> ». There is a ***genre*** <span style="color:green">[kind, genus – genre: a sort, but also the logician's genus]</span> of science, or a treatment of science, that seems very difficult to classify, and whose history it is even difficult to follow. These are not « ***techniques*** <span style="color:green">[techniques, crafts – the French for what Massumi renders "technologies", which imports machines and industry]</span> », according to the customary ***acception*** <span style="color:green">[acceptation, accepted sense of a word]</span>. But they are not « sciences » either, in the royal or legal sense established by history. According to a recent book by Michel Serres, one can ***repérer la trace*** <span style="color:green">[pick out the trace – repérer: to locate by landmarks, to spot; Massumi: "are marked by it", which makes the sciences carry the mark rather than Serres find it]</span> of it at once in atomic physics, from Democritus to Lucretius, and in the geometry of Archimedes.¹⁴ The characters of such an *excentrique* science would be the following: 1) It would first have a ***modèle hydraulique*** <span style="color:green">[hydraulic model – from hudōr, water: fluids as the general case; cf. the hydraulic despotism of Wittfogel in plateau 13, where the State captures the same waters]</span>, instead of being a theory of solids considering fluids as a particular case; in effect, ancient atomism is not separable from *flux*, *flux* is reality itself or ***consistance*** <span style="color:green">[consistency – solidity, holding-together; the plane of consistency of ATP, where heterogeneous elements hold together without a form]</span>. 2) It is a model of becoming and heterogeneity, which opposes itself to the stable, to the eternal, to the identical, to the constant. It is a « paradox », to make becoming itself a model, and no longer the ***caractère second d'une copie*** <span style="color:green">[the secondary character of a copy – becoming as what a copy has, in Plato, by not being the model; Massumi: "a secondary characteristic, a copy", which splits the phrase in two]</span>; Plato, in the <u>Timaeus</u>, evoked this possibility, but to exclude it and *conjurer* it, in the name of royal science. Now, in atomism, on the contrary, the famous ***déclinaison*** <span style="color:green">[declination – Lucretius's clinamen, the swerve of the atom from the vertical; also grammatical declension and the declination of a compass]</span> of the atom furnishes such a model of heterogeneity, and of passage or of becoming in the heterogeneous.<br>Notes: ¹⁴ [Minn: 16, Fr: 14] Michel Serres, <u>La naissance de la physique dans le texte de Lucrèce. Fleuves et turbulences</u>, Éd. de Minuit. Serres is the first to *dégager* the three points that follow; the fourth seems to us to link up with them. [English: <u>The Birth of Physics</u>, trans. Jack Hawkes, Clinamen, 2000.] |
| 12¶24 fr.446 umn.361-62 | Le clinamen, comme angle minimal, n'a de sens qu'entre une droite et une courbe, la courbe et sa tangente, et constitue la courbure première du mouvement de l'atome. Le clinamen, c'est le plus petit angle par lequel l'atome s'écarte de la droite. C'est un passage à la limite, une exhaustion, un modèle « exhaustif » paradoxal. Il en est de même dans la géométrie d'Archimède, où la droite définie comme « le plus court chemin d'un point à un autre », n'est qu'un moyen de définir la longueur d'une courbe, dans un calcul prédifférentiel. 3) On ne va plus de la droite à ses parallèles, dans un écoulement lamellaire ou laminaire, mais de la déclinaison curviligne à la formation des spirales et tourbillons sur un plan incliné : la plus grande pente pour le plus petit angle. De la turba ou turbo : c'est-à-dire des bandes ou meutes d'atomes aux grandes organisations tourbillonnaires. Le modèle est tourbillonnaire, dans un espace ouvert où les choses-flux se distribuent, au lieu de distribuer un espace fermé pour des choses linéaires et solides. C'est la différence entre un espace lisse (vectoriel, projectif ou topologique) et un espace strié (métrique) : dans un cas « on occupe l'espace sans le compter », dans l'autre cas « on le compte pour l'occuper 15 ». 4) Enfin le modèle est problématique, et non plus théorématique : les figures ne sont considérées qu'en fonction des affections qui leur arrivent, sections, ablations, adjonctions, projections. On ne va pas d'un genre à ses espèces, par différences spécifiques, ni d'une essence stable aux propriétés qui en découlent, par déduction, mais d'un problème aux accidents qui le conditionnent et le résolvent. | The clinamen, as the minimum angle, has meaning only between a straight line and a curve, the curve and its tangent, and constitutes the original curvature of the movement of the atom. The clinamen is the smallest angle by which an atom deviates from a straight path.¹⁷ It is a passage to the limit, an exhaustion, a paradoxical "exhaustive" model. The same applies to Archimedean geometry, in which the straight line, defined as "the shortest path between two points," is just a way of defining the length of a curve in a predifferential calculus. 3. One no longer goes from the straight line to its parallels, in a lamellar or laminar flow,¹⁸ but from a curvilinear declination to the formation of spirals and vortices on an inclined plane: the greatest slope for the smallest angle. From turba to turbo: in other words, from bands or packs of atoms to the great vortical organizations.¹⁹ The model is a vortical one; it operates in an open space throughout which things-flows are distributed, rather than plotting out a closed space for linear and solid things. It is the difference between a smooth (vectorial, projective, or topological) space and a striated (metric) space: in the first case "space is occupied without being counted," and in the second case "space is counted in order to be occupied."²⁰ 4. Finally, the model is problematic, rather than theorematic: figures are considered only from the viewpoint of the affections that befall them: sections, ablations, adjunctions, projections. One does not go by specific differences from a genus to its species, or by deduction from a stable essence to the properties deriving from it, but rather from a problem to the accidents that condition and resolve it. | The ***clinamen*** <span style="color:green">[Latin: the swerve – Lucretius's word for the atom's minimal deviation, De rerum natura II]</span>, as minimal angle, has sense only between a straight line and a curve, the curve and its tangent, and constitutes the ***courbure première*** <span style="color:green">[first curvature, primary curvature – first in order, not in time; Massumi: "original curvature", which suggests an origin]</span> of the movement of the atom. The *clinamen* is the smallest angle by which the atom ***s'écarte*** <span style="color:green">[swerves aside, deviates – s'écarter: to step aside, to move apart]</span> from the straight line. It is a passage to the limit, an ***exhaustion*** <span style="color:green">[exhaustion – the Greek method of exhaustion (Eudoxus, Archimedes): approaching a curve by inscribed polygons whose sides shrink without end]</span>, a paradoxical « exhaustive » model. It is the same in the geometry of Archimedes, where the straight line, defined as « the shortest path from one point to another », is only a means of defining the length of a curve, in a ***calcul prédifférentiel*** <span style="color:green">[predifferential calculus – the methods before Leibniz and Newton that already measured curves by limits]</span>. 3) One no longer goes from the straight line to its parallels, in an ***écoulement lamellaire ou laminaire*** <span style="color:green">[lamellar or laminar flow – fluid mechanics: flow in parallel layers that do not mix, against turbulent flow]</span>, but from the curvilinear *déclinaison* to the formation of spirals and ***tourbillons*** <span style="color:green">[whirlpools, vortices – eddies in water or air; Descartes's tourbillons of matter]</span> on an ***plan incliné*** <span style="color:green">[inclined plane – the slope down which the atoms fall and swirl; Massumi: "inclined plane"]</span>: the greatest slope for the smallest angle. From the ***turba*** <span style="color:green">[Latin: crowd, throng, disorder – Serres's pairing of the mob and the whirl]</span> or ***turbo*** <span style="color:green">[Latin: whirlwind, spinning top, vortex]</span>: that is to say from the bands or *meutes* of atoms to the great ***tourbillonnaires*** <span style="color:green">[vortical, whirling – the adjective of tourbillon]</span> organizations. The model is *tourbillonnaire*, in an open space where ***choses-flux*** <span style="color:green">[things-flows – things that are flows, hyphenated as one]</span> *se distribuent*, instead of distributing a closed space for linear and solid things. It is the difference between a *lisse* space (***vectoriel, projectif ou topologique*** <span style="color:green">[vectorial, projective or topological – geometries without fixed measure: direction, projection, continuity]</span>) and a *strié* space (***métrique*** <span style="color:green">[metric – measured by distances and units]</span>): in the one case « one occupies space without counting it », in the other case « one counts it in order to occupy it ».¹⁵ 4) Finally the model is ***problématique*** <span style="color:green">[problematic – proceeding by problems, as in Difference and Repetition's Ideas-problems; not doubtful]</span>, and no longer ***théorématique*** <span style="color:green">[theorematic – proceeding by theorems deduced from axioms, the Euclidean order]</span>: figures are considered only as a function of the ***affections*** <span style="color:green">[affections – what happens to a body or figure; Spinoza's affectiones, the modifications a thing undergoes]</span> that happen to them, sections, ablations, adjunctions, projections. One does not go from a genus to its species, by specific differences, nor from a stable essence to the properties that flow from it, by deduction, but from a problem to the ***accidents*** <span style="color:green">[accidents – logic: what befalls a thing without belonging to its essence; here they condition the problem]</span> that condition it and resolve it. |
| 12¶25 fr.446 umn.362 | Il y a là toutes sortes de déformations, de transmutations, de passages à la limite, d'opérations où chaque figure désigne un « événement » beau[...] 15. C'est Pierre Boulez qui distingue ainsi deux espaces-temps de la musique : dans l'espace strié, la mesure peut être irrégulière aussi bien que régulière, elle est toujours assignable, tandis que, pour l'espace lisse, la coupure, ou l'écart, « sera libre de s'effectuer où l'on veut ». Cf. Penser la musique aujourd'hui, Gonthier, pp. 95-107. [...] coup plus qu'une essence : le carré n'existe plus indépendamment d'une quadrature, le cube d'une cubature, la droite d'une rectification. Tandis que le théorème est de l'ordre des raisons, le problème est affectif, et inséparable des métamorphoses, générations et créations dans la science elle-même. Contrairement à ce que dit Gabriel Marcel, le problème n'est pas un « obstacle », c'est le franchissement de l'obstacle, une pro-jection, c'est-à-dire une machine de guerre. C'est tout ce mouvement que la science royale s'efforce de limiter, quand elle réduit le plus possible la part de l'« élément-problème », et le subordonne à l'« élément-théorème 16 ». Cette science archimédienne, ou cette conception de la science, est essentiellement liée à la machine de guerre : les problemata sont la machine de guerre elle-même, et ne sont pas séparables des plans inclinés, des passages à la limite, des tourbillons et projections. On dirait que la machine de guerre se projette dans un savoir abstrait, formellement différent de celui qui double l'appareil d'Etat. On dirait que toute une science nomade se développe excentriquement, très différente des sciences royales ou impériales. | This involves all kinds of deformations, transmutations, passages to the limit, operations in which each figure designates an "event" much more than an essence; the square no longer exists independently of a quadrature, the cube of a cubature, the straight line of a rectification. Whereas the theorem belongs to the rational order, the problem is affective and is inseparable from the metamorphoses, generations, and creations within science itself. Despite what Gabriel Marcel may say, the problem is not an "obstacle"; it is the surpassing of the obstacle, a pro-jection, in other words, a war machine. All of this movement is what royal science is striving to limit when it reduces as much as possible the range of the "problem-element" and subordinates it to the "theorem-element."²¹ This Archimedean science, or this conception of science, is bound up in an essential way with the war machine: the problemata are the war machine itself and are inseparable from inclined planes, passages to the limit, vortices, and projections. It would seem that the war machine is projected into an abstract knowledge formally different from the one that doubles the State apparatus. It would seem that a whole nomad science develops eccentrically, one that is very different from the royal or imperial sciences. | There are here all sorts of deformations, of transmutations, of passages to the limit, of operations where each figure designates an « ***événement*** <span style="color:green">[event – the event of The Logic of Sense: what happens to bodies, against the essence they have]</span> » much more than an essence: the square no longer exists independently of a ***quadrature*** <span style="color:green">[quadrature – the operation of squaring: constructing a square equal in area to a given figure, as in squaring the circle]</span>, the cube of a ***cubature*** <span style="color:green">[cubature – computing a volume, constructing a cube of equal volume]</span>, the straight line of a ***rectification*** <span style="color:green">[rectification – geometry: finding a straight line equal in length to a curve]</span>. Whereas the theorem is ***de l'ordre des raisons*** <span style="color:green">[of the order of reasons – Descartes's ordre des raisons, the chain of deduction (Guéroult's Descartes selon l'ordre des raisons, 1953); Massumi: "belongs to the rational order"]</span>, the problem is ***affectif*** <span style="color:green">[affective – bound to affections (12¶24): what happens to figures, not feelings]</span>, and inseparable from the metamorphoses, generations, and creations in science itself. Contrary to what Gabriel Marcel says, the problem is not an « obstacle », it is the ***franchissement*** <span style="color:green">[crossing, clearing – franchir: to cross over, to leap a barrier; Massumi: "surpassing"]</span> of the obstacle, a ***pro-jection*** <span style="color:green">[pro-jection – Latin pro-jacere, to throw forward, like the Greek problēma, a thing thrown forward or set before]</span>, that is to say a *machine de guerre*. It is this whole movement that royal science strives to limit, when it reduces as much as possible the part of the « problem-element », and subordinates it to the « theorem-element ».¹⁶ This Archimedean science, or this conception of science, is essentially bound to the *machine de guerre*: the ***problemata*** <span style="color:green">[Greek: problems, things thrown forward – also projecting shields and barriers, the defensive works of military engineering]</span> are the *machine de guerre* itself, and are not separable from inclined planes, passages to the limit, *tourbillons*, and projections. *On dirait* that the *machine de guerre* projects itself into an abstract ***savoir*** <span style="color:green">[knowledge – savoir, the body of knowledge, against connaissance (Foucault's distinction)]</span>, formally different from that which ***double*** <span style="color:green">[doubles – lines, accompanies as a lining doubles a coat, and duplicates]</span> the *appareil d'Etat*. *On dirait* that a whole nomad science develops itself *excentriquement*, very different from the royal or imperial sciences.<br>Notes: ¹⁵ [Minn: 20, Fr: 15] It is Pierre Boulez who distinguishes in this way two space-times of music: in striated space, the measure can be irregular as well as regular, it is always assignable, whereas, for smooth space, the cut, or the gap, « will be free to *s'effectuer* where one wants ». Cf. <u>Penser la musique aujourd'hui</u>, Gonthier, pp. 95-107. [English: <u>Boulez on Music Today</u>, trans. Susan Bradshaw and Richard Rodney Bennett, Harvard, 1971.] |
| 12¶26 fr.446 umn.362 | Bien plus, cette science nomade ne cesse pas d'être « barrée », inhibée ou interdite par les exigences et les conditions de la science d'Etat. Archimède, vaincu par l'Etat romain, devient un symbole 17. C'est que les deux sciences diffèrent par le mode de formalisation, et que la science d'Etat ne cesse pas d'imposer sa forme de souveraineté aux inventions de la science nomade ; elle ne retient de la science nomade que ce qu'elle peut s'approprier, et, pour le reste, elle en fait un ensemble de recettes étroitement limitées, sans statut vraiment scientifique, ou bien le réprime et l'interdit simplement. C'est comme si le « savant » de la science nomade était pris entre deux feux, celui de la machine de guerre qui l'alimente et l'inspire, celui de l'Etat qui lui impose un ordre des raisons. Le personnage de l'ingénieur [...] 16. La géométrie grecque est traversée par l'opposition de ces deux pôles, théorématique et problématique, et par le triomphe relatif du premier : Proclus, dans ses Commentaires sur le premier livre des Eléments d'Euclide (rééd. Desclée de Brouwer), analyse la différence des pôles, et l'illustre par l'opposition Speusippe-Menechme. Les mathématiques ne cesseront pas d'être traversées par cette tension ; et, par exemple, l'élément axiomatique se heurtera à un courant problématique, « intuitionniste » ou « constructiviste », qui fait valoir un calcul des problèmes très différent de l'axiomatique et de toute théorématique : cf. Bouligand, Le déclin des absolus mathématico-logiques, Ed. d'Enseignement supérieur. 17. Virilio, L'insécurité du territoire, p. 120 : « On sait comment, avec Archimède, se termina l'ère de la jeune géométrie comme libre recherche créatrice. (...) L'épée d'un soldat romain en a tranché le fil, dit la tradition. | Furthermore, this nomad science is continually "barred," inhibited, or banned by the demands and conditions of State science. Archimedes, vanquished by the Roman State, becomes a symbol.²² The fact is that the two kinds of science have different modes of formalization, and State science continually imposes its form of sovereignty on the inventions of nomad science. State science retains of nomad science only what it can appropriate; it turns the rest into a set of strictly limited formulas without any real scientific status, or else simply represses and bans it. It is as if the "savants" of nomad science were caught between a rock and a hard place, between the war machine that nourishes and inspires them and the State that imposes upon them an order of reasons. The figure of the engineer | Much more, this nomad science does not cease to be « ***barrée*** <span style="color:green">[barred – blocked, as a road is barred; also crossed out, struck through]</span> », inhibited, or forbidden by the exigencies and conditions of State science. Archimedes, vanquished by the Roman State, becomes a symbol.¹⁷ It is that the two sciences differ by the mode of formalization, and that State science does not cease to impose its form of sovereignty on the inventions of nomad science; it retains of nomad science only what it can appropriate, and, for the rest, it makes of it a set of ***recettes*** <span style="color:green">[recipes – the cook's or artisan's rules of thumb; Massumi: "formulas", which sounds more scientific than the French allows]</span> narrowly limited, without truly scientific status, or else represses it and simply forbids it. It is as if the « ***savant*** <span style="color:green">[scientist, scholar – the learned person; D&G's quotation marks mark a title the State grants or withholds]</span> » of nomad science were *pris* ***entre deux feux*** <span style="color:green">[between two fires – military: caught in a crossfire; Massumi: "between a rock and a hard place", which drops the war]</span>, that of the *machine de guerre*, which nourishes and inspires him, that of the State, which imposes on him an *ordre des raisons*. The character of the engineer<br>Notes: ¹⁶ [Minn: 21, Fr: 16] Greek geometry is traversed by the opposition of these two poles, theorematic and problematic, and by the relative triumph of the first: Proclus, in his <u>Commentaires sur le premier livre des Eléments d'Euclide</u> (repr. Desclée de Brouwer), analyzes the difference of the poles, and illustrates it by the opposition Speusippus-Menaechmus. Mathematics will not cease to be traversed by this tension; and, for example, the axiomatic element will collide with a problematic current, « intuitionist » or « constructivist », which puts forward a calculus of problems very different from the axiomatic and from every theorematic: cf. Bouligand, <u>Le déclin des absolus mathématico-logiques</u>, Éd. d'Enseignement supérieur. [English: Proclus, <u>A Commentary on the First Book of Euclid's Elements</u>, trans. Glenn R. Morrow, Princeton, 1970.] ¹⁷ [Minn: 22, Fr: 17] Virilio, <u>L'insécurité du territoire</u>, p. 120: « One knows how, with Archimedes, the era of young geometry as free creative research came to an end. (…) The sword of a Roman soldier cut its thread, says tradition. |
| 12¶27 fr.446 umn.362-63 | En tuant la création géométrique, l'Etat romain allait construire l'impérialisme géométrique d'Occident. » [...] (et notamment de l'ingénieur militaire), avec toute son ambivalence, illustre cette situation. Si bien que le plus important, c'est peut-être les phénomènes de frontière où la science nomade exerce une pression sur la science d'Etat, et où inversement la science d'Etat s'approprie et transforme les données de la science nomade. C'est vrai de l'art des camps, et de la « castramétration », qui mobilise de tout temps les projections et plans inclinés : l'Etat ne s'approprie pas cette dimension de la machine de guerre sans la soumettre à des règles civiles et métriques qui vont étroitement limiter, contrôler, localiser la science nomade, et lui interdire de développer ses conséquences à travers le champ social (Vauban à cet égard est comme la reprise d'Archimède, et subit une défaite analogue). C'est vrai de la géométrie descriptive et projective, dont la science royale veut faire une simple dépendance pratique de la géométrie analytique dite supérieure (d'où la situation ambiguë de Monge ou de Poncelet en tant que « savants 18 »). C'est vrai aussi du calcul différentiel : celui-ci n'a eu longtemps qu'un statut para-scientifique, on le traite d'« hypothèse gothique », la science royale ne lui reconnaît qu'une valeur de convention commode ou de fiction bien fondée ; les grands mathématiciens d'Etat s'efforcent de lui donner un statut plus ferme, mais précisément à condition d'en éliminer toutes les notions dynamiques et nomades comme celles de devenir, hétérogénéité, infinitésimal, passage à la limite, variation continue, etc., et de lui imposer des règles civiles, statiques et ordinales (situation ambiguë de Carnot à cet égard). | in particular the military engineer), with all its ambivalence, is illustrative of this situation. Most significant are perhaps borderline phenomena in which nomad science exerts pressure on State science, and, conversely, State science appropriates and transforms the elements of nomad science. This is true of the art of encampments, "castrametation," which has always mobilized projections and inclined planes: the State does not appropriate this dimension of the war machine without submitting it to civil and metric rules that strictly limit, control, localize nomad science, and without keeping it from having repercussions throughout the social field (in this respect, Vauban is like a repeat of Archimedes, and suffers an analogous defeat). It is true of descriptive and projective geometry, which royal science would like to turn into a mere practical dependency of analytic, or so-called higher, geometry (thus the ambiguous situation of Monge and Poncelet as "savants").²³ It is also true of differential calculus. For a long time, it had only parascientific status and was labeled a "Gothic hypothesis"; royal science only accorded it the value of a convenient convention or a well-founded fiction. The great State mathematicians did their best to improve its status, but precisely on the condition that all the dynamic, nomadic notions - such as becoming, heterogeneity, infinitesimal, passage to the limit, continuous variation - be eliminated and civil, static, and ordinal rules be imposed upon it (Carnot's ambiguous position in this respect). | (and notably of the military engineer), with all its ambivalence, illustrates this situation. So that the most important is perhaps the ***phénomènes de frontière*** <span style="color:green">[frontier phenomena – frontier: the border between States and zones; Massumi: "borderline phenomena", which loses the territory]</span> where nomad science exerts a pressure on State science, and where inversely State science appropriates and transforms the ***données*** <span style="color:green">[givens, data – what is given to be worked on; Massumi: "elements"]</span> of nomad science. This is true of the art of camps, and of « ***castramétation*** <span style="color:green">[castrametation – from castra, camp, and metari, to measure out: the laying-out of military camps; the measure is in the word]</span> », which has always mobilized projections and inclined planes: the State does not appropriate this dimension of the *machine de guerre* without submitting it to civil and metric rules that will narrowly limit, control, localize nomad science, and ***lui interdire de développer ses conséquences*** <span style="color:green">[forbid it to develop its consequences – Massumi: "without keeping it from having repercussions", which reverses the sense]</span> across the social field (Vauban in this regard is like the ***reprise*** <span style="color:green">[resumption, return – also a musical reprise and the repair of a darned fabric; Massumi: "repeat"]</span> of Archimedes, and suffers an analogous defeat). This is true of descriptive and projective geometry, which royal science wants to make into a simple practical dependency of analytic geometry, ***dite supérieure*** <span style="color:green">[so-called higher – dite again, the label of others (12¶18)]</span> (whence the ambiguous situation of Monge or of Poncelet as « *savants* »¹⁸). It is true also of the differential calculus: this long had only a para-scientific status, one treats it as « ***hypothèse gothique*** <span style="color:green">[Gothic hypothesis – the barbarous, un-classical hypothesis: Gothic as the eighteenth century's word for the medieval and irregular; cf. Worringer's Gothic line in 12¶ on metallurgy]</span> », royal science grants it only the value of a convenient convention or of a ***fiction bien fondée*** <span style="color:green">[well-founded fiction – after Leibniz's phenomena bene fundata: infinitesimals as useful fictions grounded in the real]</span>; the great mathematicians of State strive to give it a firmer status, but precisely on condition of eliminating from it all the dynamic and nomad notions like those of becoming, heterogeneity, infinitesimal, passage to the limit, continuous variation, etc., and of imposing on it civil, static, and ***ordinales*** <span style="color:green">[ordinal – of order and rank, first, second, third; set against the variable and continuous]</span> rules (ambiguous situation of Carnot in this regard).<br>Notes: ¹⁷ (cont.) By killing geometric creation, the Roman State was going to construct the geometric imperialism of the West. » |
| 12¶28 fr.449 umn.363 | C'est vrai enfin du modèle hydraulique : car, certes, l'Etat a lui-même besoin d'une science hydraulique (il n'y a pas à revenir sur les thèses de Wittfogel concernant l'importance des grands travaux hydrauliques dans un empire). Mais c'est sous une forme très différente, puisque l'Etat a besoin de subordonner la force hydraulique à des conduits, tuyaux, rives qui empêchent la turbulence, qui imposent au mouvement d'aller d'un point à un autre, à l'espace lui-même d'être strié et mesuré, au fluide de dépendre du solide, et au flux de procéder par tranches laminaires parallèles. Tandis que le modèle hydraulique de la science nomade et de la machine de guerre consiste à se répandre par turbulence dans un espace lisse, à produire un mouvement qui tient l'espace et en affecte simultanément tous les points, au lieu d'être tenu [...] 18. Avec Monge et surtout Poncelet, les limites de la représentation sensible ou même spatiale (espace strié) sont bien dépassées, mais moins vers une puissance symbolique d'abstraction que vers une imagination trans-spatiale, ou trans-intuition (continuité). On se reportera au commentaire de Brunschvicg sur Poncelet, Les étapes de la philosophie mathématique, P.U.F. [...] par lui comme dans le mouvement local qui va de tel point à tel autre point 19. Démocrite, Ménechme, Archimède, Vauban, Desargues, Bernoulli, Monge, Carnot, Poncelet, Perronet, etc. : il faut chaque fois une monographie pour rendre compte de la situation spéciale de ces savants que la science d'Etat n'utilise pas sans les restreindre, les discipliner, réprimer leurs conceptions sociales ou politiques. La mer comme espace lisse est bien un problème spécifique de la machine de guerre. | Finally, it is true of the hydraulic model, for it is certain that the State itself needs a hydraulic science (there is no going back on Wittfogel's theses on the importance of large-scale waterworks for an empire). But it needs it in a very different form, because the State needs to subordinate hydraulic force to conduits, pipes, embankments, which prevent turbulence, which constrain movement to go from one point to another, and space itself to be striated and measured, which makes the fluid depend on the solid, and flows proceed by parallel, laminar layers. The hydraulic model of nomad science and the war machine, on the other hand, consists in being distributed by turbulence across a smooth space, in producing a movement that holds space and simultaneously affects all of its points, instead of being held by space in a local movement from one specified point to another.²⁴ Democritus, Menaechmus, Archimedes, Vauban, Desargues, Bernoulli, Monge, Carnot, Poncelet, Perronet, etc.: in each case a monograph would be necessary to take into account the special situation of these savants whom State science used only after restraining or disciplining them, after repressing their social or political conceptions. The sea as a smooth space is a specific problem of the war machine. | It is true finally of the *modèle hydraulique*: for, certainly, the State itself needs a hydraulic science (there is no going back over the theses of ***Wittfogel*** <span style="color:green">[Karl Wittfogel, Oriental Despotism (1957): the hydraulic society, whose State rises from the management of great irrigation works]</span> concerning the importance of the great hydraulic works in an empire). But it is under a very different form, since the State needs to subordinate hydraulic force to conduits, pipes, ***rives*** <span style="color:green">[banks – the banks of a river; Massumi: "embankments", which makes them built]</span> that prevent turbulence, that impose on movement to go from one point to another, on space itself to be *strié* and measured, on the fluid to depend on the solid, and on *flux* to proceed by parallel laminar ***tranches*** <span style="color:green">[slices – layers cut flat, as bread is sliced]</span>. Whereas the *modèle hydraulique* of nomad science and of the *machine de guerre* consists in ***se répandre*** <span style="color:green">[spreading out, pouring forth – se répandre: to spill, to overflow, to spread like a rumor; Massumi: "being distributed", which makes it passive]</span> by turbulence in a *lisse* space, in producing a movement that *tient* space and affects all its points simultaneously, instead of being *tenu* by it as in the local movement that goes from such a point to such another point.¹⁹ Democritus, Menaechmus, Archimedes, Vauban, Desargues, Bernoulli, Monge, Carnot, Poncelet, Perronet, etc.: each time one needs a monograph to account for the special situation of these *savants*, whom State science does not use without restricting them, disciplining them, repressing their social or political conceptions. The sea as *lisse* space is indeed a specific problem of the *machine de guerre*.<br>Notes: ¹⁸ [Minn: 23, Fr: 18] With Monge and above all Poncelet, the limits of sensible or even spatial representation (*strié* space) are indeed surpassed, but less toward a symbolic power of abstraction than toward a trans-spatial imagination, or trans-intuition (continuity). One will refer to the commentary of Brunschvicg on Poncelet, <u>Les étapes de la philosophie mathématique</u>, P.U.F. |
| 12¶29 fr.449 umn.363-64 | C'est sur mer, comme le montre Virilio, que se pose le problème du fleet in being, c'est-à-dire la tâche d'occuper un espace ouvert, avec un mouvement tourbillonnaire dont l'effet peut surgir en n'importe quel point. A cet égard, les études récentes sur le rythme, sur l'origine de cette notion, ne nous semblent pas entièrement convaincantes. Car on nous dit que le rythme n'a rien à voir avec le mouvement des flots, mais désigne la « forme » en général, et plus spécialement la forme d'un mouvement « mesuré, cadencé 20 ». Pourtant, rythme et mesure ne sont jamais confondus. Et si l'atomiste Démocrite est précisément un des auteurs qui emploient rythme au sens de forme, on ne doit pas oublier que c'est dans des conditions très précises de fluctuation, et que les formes d'atomes constituent d'abord de grands ensembles non métriques, des espaces lisses tels que l'air, la mer ou même la terre (magnae res). Il y a bien un rythme mesuré, cadencé qui renvoie à l'écoulement du fleuve entre ses rives ou à la forme d'un espace strié ; mais il y a aussi un rythme sans mesure, qui renvoie à la fluxion d'un flux, c'est-à-dire à la façon dont un fluide occupe un espace lisse. Cette opposition ou plutôt cette tension-limite des deux sciences, science nomade de machine de guerre et science royale d'Etat, se retrouve à différents moments, à différents niveaux. Les travaux d'Anne Querrien permettent de repérer deux de ces moments, l'un avec la construction des cathédrales gothiques au [...] 19. Michel Serres (pp. 105 sq.) analyse à cet égard l'opposition d'Alembert-Bernoulli. Il s'agit plus généralement d'une différence entre deux modèles d'espace : « Le bassin méditerranéen manque d'eau, et celui qui tient le pouvoir est celui qui draine les eaux. | As Virilio shows, it is at sea that the problem of the fleet in being is posed, in other words, the task of occupying an open space with a vortical movement that can rise up at any point. In this respect, the recent studies on rhythm, on the origin of that notion, do not seem entirely convincing. For we are told that rhythm has nothing to do with the movement of waves but rather that it designates "form" in general, and more specifically the form of a "measured, cadenced" movement.²⁵ However, rhythm is never the same as measure.
